<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> We construct a singular normal affine complex surface whose tangent sheaf is free of rank two. This disproves the Lipman–Zariski conjecture in characteristic zero.

- **C1** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exist a finitely generated normal integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$ of Krull dimension two and a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m\subset A$ such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^{\oplus2}, \qquad A_{\mathfrak m}\text{ is not regular}.$$
  - Explanation: A singular normal affine complex surface has a free derivation module, contradicting the proposed implication from tangent freeness to regularity.
- **C2** (construction, [source](#stmt-curve-six-vectors)): In a two-dimensional nondegenerate complex quadratic space, one can choose three distinct nonisotropic directions, avoiding any prescribed finite set of projective directions, and nonzero vectors $w_1,\ldots,w_6$ that use each of these directions twice, with the two vectors in each pair equal, such that $$\label{curve:vector-conditions} w=\sum_{j=1}^6w_j,\qquad w^2=90,\qquad w_j^2+w_j\mathbin{\cdot}w=1 \quad(1\le j\le6).$$
  - Explanation: Paired vectors in a complex quadratic space meet the pointwise normalization and total norm requirements while avoiding prescribed directions.
- **C3** (theorem, [source](#stmt-curve-adjoint-jets)): For $0\le j\le3$, set $$\mathcal A_j=\Gamma+(4-j)F=(6-j,2),\qquad N_j=\mathcal A_j\|_\Gamma=(8-j)F+\Delta.$$ Restriction identifies $H^0(\Sigma,\mathcal A_j)$ with $H^0(C,K_C-jH)$ and maps it onto $H^0(\Gamma,N_j)$. For $1\le\ell\le4$, evaluation on the length-$\ell$ subschemes at the six marked points has the same rank on $C$ as on $\Gamma$. The ranks needed here are $$\label{curve:residual-table} \begin{array}{c\|c\|c\|c\|c\|c} j&\ell& h^0(\Gamma,N_j)&N_j-\ell Z &h^0(\Gamma,N_j-\ell Z)&\text{evaluation rank}\\ \hline 0&1&16&5F&10&6\\ 1&2&14&\Phi&2&12\\ 2&2&12&\Delta&0&12\\ 3&2&10&-F+\Delta&0&10 \end{array}$$
  - Explanation: Ambient adjoint sections transfer evaluation ranks between the curve and the elliptic model through their contact.
- **C4** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-curve-data)): The construction above produces a smooth connected projective curve $C$, a reduced divisor $Z=q_1+\cdots+q_6$, line bundles $H,L,\theta$, and a basis $u=(u_1,u_2)$ of $H^0(C,H)$ with the following properties.* 1. *The numerical and canonical data are $$\begin{gathered} g(C)=21,\qquad \deg H=4,\qquad\deg L=10,\\ L=H+Z,\qquad\theta=2L,\qquad K_C=4L, \end{gathered}$$ where the last isomorphism is normalized by <a href="#curve:adjunction-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:adjunction-normalization">[curve:adjunction-normalization]</a> and $\xi^4\mapsto\gamma\|_C$. The curve $C$ has exactly fourth-order contact with the auxiliary elliptic curve $\Gamma$ at every $q_j$.* 2. *The pencil $u$ defines a finite degree-four morphism $f:C\to\mathbb P^1$ unramified at every $q_j$, and the values $f(q_j)$ are not all equal. The zero divisor of $u_1$ can be chosen to be a reduced fiber disjoint from $Z$.* 3. *The section dimensions are $$\label{curve:section-dimensions} \begin{gathered} h^0(C,jH)=j+1\quad(0\le j\le3),\\ h^0(C,L)=h^0(C,H+2Z)=2,\qquad h^0(C,\theta)=3,\qquad h^0(C,\mathcal O_C(Z))=1,\\ h^0(C,\theta+H)=6. \end{gathered}$$ More precisely, $$\label{curve:section-multiplication} \begin{split} H^0(C,L)&=\xi H^0(C,H),\\ H^0(C,H+2Z)&=\xi^2H^0(C,H),\\ H^0(C,\theta)&=\xi^2H^0(C,2H). \end{split}$$ In particular $r=\xi u$ is a basis of $H^0(C,L)$ with simple common zero divisor exactly $Z$, and every section of $\theta$ vanishes twice along $Z$.* 4. *For every $k\ge2$, the evaluation map $$\label{curve:evaluation-surjectivity} H^0(C,(k+1)L)\longrightarrow H^0(Z,((k+1)L)\|_Z)$$ is surjective.* 5. *The auxiliary data on $\Gamma\subset\Sigma$, including the compatible bundle identities, the logarithmic volume $\Omega$, and the quadratic form $Q$, are as in <a href="#curve:bundle-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:bundle-identities">[curve:bundle-identities]</a> and <a href="#curve:quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:quadratic">[curve:quadratic]</a>; the divisor $Z$ belongs to $\|M\|$. The six nonzero vectors $w_j\in H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)^*$ have the projective evaluation directions at $q_j$, use three distinct directions, and satisfy <a href="#curve:vector-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:vector-conditions">[curve:vector-conditions]</a>, with $w^2=90=9\deg L$.
  - Explanation: The marked projective curve supplies the canonical root, section dimensions and interpolation needed for gluing.
- **C5** (theorem, [source](#stmt-trace-pairing)): The rank-four vector bundle $E=f_*\Lambda$ has a perfect symmetric pairing $$\label{trace:form} E\otimes E\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1},\qquad (T_1,T_2)\longmapsto \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}_{C/\mathbb P^1}\!\left(\frac{T_1T_2}{\eta_C}\right).$$ Its splitting type is $(3,0,0,-3)$. Multiplication by $\xi^2$ identifies $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(3)$ with its positive line subbundle $\mathcal P$. The form on global sections has radical $\xi^2H^0(C,3H)$, and induces a nondegenerate symmetric form $\langle\ ,\ \rangle$ on $$\label{trace:W} W=H^0(C,\Lambda)/\xi^2H^0(C,3H),\qquad \dim W=2.$$ For every $x\in\mathbb P^1$, evaluation identifies this quadratic space with $(\mathcal P^\perp/\mathcal P)_x$.* *Suppose $q\in Z$, and choose local frames $\mathfrak f$ of $H$ and $\tau$ of $\Lambda$, and a parameter $z$ at $q$. Write, using the fixed isomorphism $2\Lambda\simeq K_C+2H$, $$\tau^2=s(z)\,dz\,\mathfrak f^2, \qquad \eta_C=\mathcal E(z)\,dz\,\mathfrak f^2.$$ Coefficient evaluation $\ell_q:T\mapsto(T/\tau)(q)$ descends to $W$. Its square in the dual form is $$\label{trace:dual-evaluation} \ell_q^2=\frac{\mathcal E(q)}{s(q)}.$$ In particular, it is nonzero and nonisotropic.
  - Explanation: A perfect trace pairing induces a nondegenerate form on the quotient of global sections and determines dual evaluation norms.
- **C6** (theorem, [source](#stmt-trace-principal-parts)): There is an isomorphism $$\label{trace:iota} \iota:W\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)$$ that identifies the full principal parts of $T/\xi^2$ on $C$ with those of $\iota([T])/\beta^2$ on $\Gamma$ through residue duality. It identifies the projective evaluations at corresponding points of $Z$. Moreover, its dual $$\iota^*:H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)^*\longrightarrow W^*$$ is an isometry for the quadratic form $Q$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:curve-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:curve-data">4</a> and the dual trace form.
  - Explanation: Residue duality identifies full principal parts and preserves the quadratic form with its scalar and sign.
- **C7** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-residue-data)): For the curve, line bundles, fixed fourth-root isomorphism, and six vectors $w_j\in H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)^*$ of Proposition <a href="#prop:curve-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:curve-data">4</a>, one can choose the basis $u$ with unchanged determinant so that $(u_1)_0$ is an unramified fiber of $f$ disjoint from $Z$. Set $r=\xi u$ and define $$v_j^*=\iota^*w_j\in W^*,\qquad V=\sum_{j=1}^6v_j^*.$$ For any mutually disjoint compatible coordinate disks as above there are nonzero constants $c_j$ and a global pair $$P=(P_1,P_2)\in H^0(C,\theta+Z)^{\oplus2}$$ with the following properties. First, $$\label{trace:vector-identities} c_jl_j=v_j^*,\qquad V^2=90=9\deg L,\qquad (v_j^*)^2+v_j^*\cdot V=1.$$ On disk $j$, using coefficient rows in the indicated frames, put $$\label{trace:local-data} m_j=\frac{c_j}{z_j},\qquad R_j=m_jr,\qquad W_j=R_j(0)\wedge R_j'(0),\qquad p_j=P-m_jr'.$$ The pair $P$ has principal part $R_j(0)/z_j$ in the frame $(\ell_j^{\mathrm{fr}})^2$ of $\theta$; the rows $R_j,p_j$ are holomorphic on the disk, and $R_j(0)\ne0$. Moreover, $$\label{trace:residue-identities} W_j=(v_j^*)^2,\qquad p_j(0)\wedge R_j(0)=1,\qquad \sum_{j=1}^6(W_j-1)=-9\deg L.$$ Globally, $$\label{trace:J} J=u\wedge P\in H^0(C,\Lambda),\qquad \langle[J],[T]\rangle=-V([T]) \quad\text{for every }T\in H^0(C,\Lambda).$$ Changing $P$ by a holomorphic pair of sections of $\theta$ does not change $[J]$. The pole prescription is consistent under changes of compatible coordinates.
  - Explanation: A meromorphic pair realizes the prescribed poles and cancels the global residue sums while enforcing the local determinant normalization.
- **C8** (theorem, [source](#stmt-jets-bracket)): For $D$ as in <a href="#jets:generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jets:generator">[jets:generator]</a> and $V=t^{\ell+1}(AE_t+B\partial_z)$ one has $$\begin{aligned} =t^{k+\ell+1}\bigl(&((\ell+1-k)bA+hA'-Bb')E_t\notag\\ &+((\ell+1)bB+hB'-khA-Bh')\partial_z\bigr). \label{jets:general-bracket} \end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\label{jets:divergence} \mathcal L_D\sigma=t^k\bigl((k-4)b+h'\bigr)\sigma.$$ In particular, for $k\ne4$ the choice $b=h'/(4-k)$ preserves $\sigma$ exactly. The first effect of an order-$k$ modification on <a href="#jets:fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jets:fields">[jets:fields]</a> is $$\label{jets:leading-action} \Delta A_k=(1-k)br+hr',\qquad \Delta B_k=-khr.$$
  - Explanation: The source states bracket, divergence and leading-action formulas for flow generators acting on vector-field coefficients.
- **C9** (theorem, [source](#stmt-jets-second-obstruction)): The order-two vertical obstruction vanishes.
  - Explanation: The low-order vertical gluing class vanishes after its residue pairings are evaluated.
- **C10** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-jets)): For the data of Propositions <a href="#prop:curve-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:curve-data">4</a> and <a href="#prop:residue-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:residue-data">7</a>, there are finitely many holomorphic disk-to-outside transition germs, each a composition of flows <a href="#jets:generator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jets:generator">[jets:generator]</a> of orders $1,\ldots,7$, together with holomorphic local rows $A_k,B_k$ for $0\leq k\leq7$, such that: the transitions fix $C$ and preserve its conormal bundle $L$; the vector fields <a href="#jets:fields" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="jets:fields">[jets:fields]</a> match through row index seven; and $A\wedge B=t^2(1+O(t))$ on every chart.* *On any surface neighborhood $Y$ realizing these transition germs, write $\mathcal I=\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\mathcal F=\mathcal T_Y(-\log C)$. The rows give two sections of $\mathcal F/\mathcal I^9\mathcal F$, lying in $\mathcal I\mathcal F/\mathcal I^9\mathcal F$. Their determinant has leading term $t^4E_t\wedge\partial_z$, equivalently $t^5\partial_t\wedge\partial_z$, with coefficient one.
  - Explanation: A finite sequence of overlap flows yields compatible vector-field jets with the required leading determinant.
- **C11** (construction, [source](#stmt-analytic-realization)): The overlap maps of Proposition <a href="#prop:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:jets">10</a> define, after shrinking their domains, a smooth Hausdorff complex surface neighborhood $Y$ of $C$. Its conormal bundle is $L$, and hence $$C^2=-10.$$ Writing $\mathcal I=\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\mathcal F=\mathcal T_Y(-\log C)$, the prescribed rows define two global sections $$\bar v_1,\bar v_2\in H^0(Y,\mathcal F/\mathcal I^9\mathcal F).$$ In divisor coordinates $(t,z)$, their local representatives have logarithmic determinant $t^4(1+O(t))$ and ordinary determinant $t^5(1+O(t))$.
  - Explanation: The finite overlap maps realize a smooth Hausdorff neighborhood with the prescribed conormal bundle and quotient sections.
- **C12** (theorem, [source](#stmt-analytic-graded-vanishing)): For every $n\geq9$, $$H^1(C,\mathcal F\|_C\otimes L^n)=0.$$ Consequently, for $\mathcal E=\mathcal I^9\mathcal F$ and every $r\geq1$, $$H^1(Y,\mathcal E/\mathcal I^r\mathcal E)=0.$$
  - Explanation: Twisted logarithmic tangent cohomology vanishes in the tail, as does the cohomology of each finite ideal quotient.
- **C13** (theorem, [source](#stmt-analytic-actual-lifting)): After shrinking $Y$ about $C$, the sections $\bar v_1,\bar v_2$ lift to holomorphic sections $v_1,v_2$ of $\mathcal F$. They form a frame for $\mathcal T_{Y\setminus C}$.
  - Explanation: The quotient sections lift holomorphically after shrinking and frame the tangent sheaf away from the exceptional curve.
- **C14** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-prop-analytic-counterexample)): There is a normal complex surface germ $(X,x)$ with an isolated singular point and free analytic derivation sheaf of rank two: $$\mathcal T_X=\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal O_X}(\Omega_X^1,\mathcal O_X) \simeq\mathcal O_X^{\oplus2}.$$ It has a resolution $\pi:Y\to X$ whose exceptional set is the smooth curve $C$ of genus $21$ and self-intersection $-10$.
  - Explanation: Contracting the exceptional curve produces an isolated normal surface singularity with a free analytic derivation sheaf.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-analytic-invariants)): The construction also determines its canonical discrepancy and a lower bound for its geometric genus. The two-form $\omega^\circ$ dual to the frame $w_1,w_2$ generates the canonical sheaf on $X^\circ$. Extending reflexively across the normal surface point gives $\omega_X\simeq\mathcal O_X$; equivalently, we may choose the canonical divisor $K_X=0$. Since a normal surface is Cohen–Macaulay, this reflexive canonical sheaf is its dualizing sheaf, so $X$ is also Gorenstein; this surface criterion is recalled in [[FlennerZaidenberg2001](#ref-FlennerZaidenberg2001), 2.4, Definition 2.18 and the following paragraph]. By <a href="#analytic:ordinary-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:ordinary-determinant">[analytic:ordinary-determinant]</a>, its pullback is $$\pi^*\omega^\circ=t^{-5}u(t,z)^{-1}\,dt\wedge dz.$$ It is meromorphic on $Y$ with divisor exactly $-5C$. Thus $$K_Y=\pi^*K_X-5C,$$ so the discrepancy of $C$ is $-5$. For the geometric genus, put $\mathcal O_{nC}=\mathcal O_Y/\mathcal I^n$. Riemann–Roch and the established equality $h^0(C,L)=2$ give $$h^1(C,L)=h^0(C,L^3)=12.$$ In the sequence $$0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{2C} \longrightarrow\mathcal O_C\longrightarrow0,$$ the constant section of $\mathcal O_C$ lifts to $\mathcal O_{2C}$. Since $H^2(C,L)=0$, its cohomology sequence gives $$h^1(\mathcal O_{2C})=h^1(C,L)+h^1(C,\mathcal O_C)=12+21=33.$$ For every $n\geq1$, the sequence with kernel $L^n$ similarly shows that $H^1(\mathcal O_{(n+1)C})\to H^1(\mathcal O_{nC})$ is surjective. The theorem on formal functions, with the cofinal filtrations in <a href="#analytic:cofinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:cofinal">[analytic:cofinal]</a>, identifies their inverse limit with the completion of $(R^1\pi_*\mathcal O_Y)_x$. This stalk has finite length, since the coherent higher direct image is supported at $x$, and thus equals its completion. The inverse limit maps onto $H^1(\mathcal O_{2C})$, proving $$p_g(X,x)=\dim_\mathbb C(R^1\pi_*\mathcal O_Y)_x\geq33.$$ The exceptional curve has genus $g=21$ and its dual graph has first Betti number $b=0$. Consequently $p_g-g-b\geq12$.
  - Explanation: The analytic singularity is Gorenstein; its canonical discrepancy and geometric-genus bound place it beyond the cited smoothness criteria.
- **C16** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy)): Suppose that $T^1$ in <a href="#algebra:conormal-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:conormal-dual">[algebra:conormal-dual]</a> has finite length. There exist integers $N,n_0$ depending only on $f,D$ such that the following holds. If a row $g\in P^s$ and an $s\times t$ matrix $B$ satisfy $$gB=0,\qquad g-f\in\mathfrak n^{n_0}P^s,\qquad B-D\in\mathfrak n^NP^{s\times t},$$ then there are a continuous $\mathbb C$-algebra automorphism $\Phi$ of $P$ and an invertible matrix $U\in\operatorname{GL}_s(P)$ such that $$f=\Phi(g)U.$$ One can take $\Phi(x_j)\equiv x_j\pmod{\mathfrak n^2}$ and $U\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak n}$. Consequently $P/(g)\simeq P/(f)$ as complete local $\mathbb C$-algebras.
  - Explanation: Finite length of the conormal obstruction quotient gives formal equivalence for sufficiently close equations and relation matrices with an exact zero product.
- **C17** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-algebraization)): Let $(X,x)$ be a normal complex analytic germ of dimension $d\ge1$, smooth away from $x$, and suppose that its holomorphic tangent module is free of rank $d$. There exist a finitely generated normal integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$ and a maximal ideal $\mathfrak p\subset A$ such that $$\dim A=d,\qquad A/\mathfrak p=\mathbb C,\qquad \widehat{A_{\mathfrak p}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal O_{X,x}},\qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^d.$$ The affine variety $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ can be chosen smooth away from $\mathfrak p$. If $x$ is singular, $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is nonregular.
  - Explanation: An isolated normal analytic germ with free tangent module admits a normal affine algebraization preserving its completion and tangent freeness.
- **C18** (theorem, [source](#stmt-algebra-log-kodaira)): The smooth locus $U$ of the affine surface $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ constructed in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has $\overline\kappa(U)=2$.
  - Explanation: The constructed affine surface has smooth locus of maximal logarithmic Kodaira dimension.
- **C19** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups)): Starting with the singular surface $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, every iteration of blowing up at the derivation module is isomorphic to $X$. The same holds if each blowup is followed by normalization. Neither procedure resolves its singular point.
  - Explanation: Derivation-module blowups, with or without subsequent normalization, leave this singular surface unchanged.

        "anchor": "stmt-trace-pairing",
        "exact_quote": "The rank-four vector bundle $E=f_*\\Lambda$ has a perfect symmetric pairing $$\\label{trace:form}\n E\\otimes E\\longrightarrow\\mathcal O_{\\mathbb P^1},\\qquad\n (T_1,T_2)\\longmapsto\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{Tr}}_{C/\\mathbb P^1}\\!\\left(\\frac{T_1T_2}{\\eta_C}\\right).$$ Its splitting type is $(3,0,0,-3)$. Multiplication by $\\xi^2$ identifies $\\mathcal O_{\\mathbb P^1}(3)$ with its positive line subbundle $\\mathcal P$. The form on global sections has radical $\\xi^2H^0(C,3H)$, and induces a nondegenerate symmetric form $\\langle\\ ,\\ \\rangle$ on $$\\label{trace:W}\n W=H^0(C,\\Lambda)/\\xi^2H^0(C,3H),\\qquad \\dim W=2.$$ For every $x\\in\\mathbb P^1$, evaluation identifies this quadratic space with $(\\mathcal P^\\perp/\\mathcal P)_x$.*\n\n\n*Suppose $q\\in Z$, and choose local frames $\\mathfrak f$ of $H$ and $\\tau$ of $\\Lambda$, and a parameter $z$ at $q$. Write, using the fixed isomorphism $2\\Lambda\\simeq K_C+2H$, $$\\tau^2=s(z)\\,dz\\,\\mathfrak f^2,\n \\qquad \\eta_C=\\mathcal E(z)\\,dz\\,\\mathfrak f^2.$$ Coefficient evaluation $\\ell_q:T\\mapsto(T/\\tau)(q)$ descends to $W$. Its square in the dual form is $$\\label{trace:dual-evaluation}\n \\ell_q^2=\\frac{\\mathcal E(q)}{s(q)}.$$ In particular, it is nonzero and nonisotropic.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-trace-principal-parts",
        "exact_quote": "There is an isomorphism $$\\label{trace:iota}\n \\iota:W\\xrightarrow{\\ \\sim\\ }H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)$$ that identifies the full principal parts of $T/\\xi^2$ on $C$ with those of $\\iota([T])/\\beta^2$ on $\\Gamma$ through residue duality. It identifies the projective evaluations at corresponding points of $Z$. Moreover, its dual $$\\iota^*:H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)^*\\longrightarrow W^*$$ is an isometry for the quadratic form $Q$ of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a> and the dual trace form.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-trace-principal-parts",
          "exact_quote": "There is an isomorphism $$\\label{trace:iota}\n \\iota:W\\xrightarrow{\\ \\sim\\ }H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)$$ that identifies the full principal parts of $T/\\xi^2$ on $C$ with those of $\\iota([T])/\\beta^2$ on $\\Gamma$ through residue duality. It identifies the projective evaluations at corresponding points of $Z$. Moreover, its dual $$\\iota^*:H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)^*\\longrightarrow W^*$$ is an isometry for the quadratic form $Q$ of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a> and the dual trace form.",
          "source_id": "PM-048.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is an isomorphism $$\\label{trace:iota}\n \\iota:W\\xrightarrow{\\ \\sim\\ }H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)$$ that identifies the full principal parts of $T/\\xi^2$ on $C$ with those of $\\iota([T])/\\beta^2$ on $\\Gamma$ through residue duality.       "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-residue-data",
        "exact_quote": "For the curve, line bundles, fixed fourth-root isomorphism, and six vectors $w_j\\in H^0(\\Gamma,\\Phi)^*$ of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a>, one can choose the basis $u$ with unchanged determinant so that $(u_1)_0$ is an unramified fiber of $f$ disjoint from $Z$. Set $r=\\xi u$ and define $$v_j^*=\\iota^*w_j\\in W^*,\\qquad V=\\sum_{j=1}^6v_j^*.$$ For any mutually disjoint compatible coordinate disks as above there are nonzero constants $c_j$ and a global pair $$P=(P_1,P_2)\\in H^0(C,\\theta+Z)^{\\oplus2}$$ with the following properties. First, $$\\label{trace:vector-identities}\n c_jl_j=v_j^*,\\qquad\n V^2=90=9\\deg L,\\qquad (v_j^*)^2+v_j^*\\cdot V=1.$$ On disk $j$, using coefficient rows in the indicated frames, put $$\\label{trace:local-data}\n m_j=\\frac{c_j}{z_j},\\qquad\n R_j=m_jr,\\qquad\n W_j=R_j(0)\\wedge R_j'(0),\\qquad\n p_j=P-m_jr'.$$ The pair $P$ has principal part $R_j(0)/z_j$ in the frame $(\\ell_j^{\\mathrm{fr}})^2$ of $\\theta$; the rows $R_j,p_j$ are holomorphic on the disk, and $R_j(0)\\ne0$. Moreover, $$\\label{trace:residue-identities}\n W_j=(v_j^*)^2,\\qquad\n p_j(0)\\wedge R_j(0)=1,\\qquad\n \\sum_{j=1}^6(W_j-1)=-9\\deg L.$$ Globally, $$\\label{trace:J}\n J=u\\wedge P\\in H^0(C,\\Lambda),\\qquad\n \\langle[J],[T]\\rangle=-V([T])\n       \\quad\\text{for every }T\\in H^0(C,\\Lambda).$$ Changing $P$ by a holomorphic pair of sections of $\\theta$ does not change $[J]$. The pole prescription is consistent under changes of compatible coordinates.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-jets-bracket",
        "exact_quote": "For $D$ as in <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> and $V=t^{\\ell+1}(AE_t+B\\partial_z)$ one has $$\\begin{aligned}\n=t^{k+\\ell+1}\\bigl(&((\\ell+1-k)bA+hA'-Bb')E_t\\notag\\\\\n &+((\\ell+1)bB+hB'-khA-Bh')\\partial_z\\bigr).\n \\label{jets:general-bracket}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\\label{jets:divergence}\n \\mathcal L_D\\sigma=t^k\\bigl((k-4)b+h'\\bigr)\\sigma.$$ In particular, for $k\\ne4$ the choice $b=h'/(4-k)$ preserves $\\sigma$ exactly. The first effect of an order-$k$ modification on <a href=\"#jets:fields\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:fields\">[jets:fields]</a> is $$\\label{jets:leading-action}\n \\Delta A_k=(1-k)br+hr',\\qquad \\Delta B_k=-khr.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-jets-bracket",
          "exact_quote": "For $D$ as in <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> and $V=t^{\\ell+1}(AE_t+B\\partial_z)$ one has $$\\begin{aligned}\n=t^{k+\\ell+1}\\bigl(&((\\ell+1-k)bA+hA'-Bb')E_t\\notag\\\\\n &+((\\ell+1)bB+hB'-khA-Bh')\\partial_z\\bigr).\n \\label{jets:general-bracket}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\\label{jets:divergence}\n \\mathcal L_D\\sigma=t^k\\bigl((k-4)b+h'\\bigr)\\sigma.$$ In particular, for $k\\ne4$ the choice $b=h'/(4-k)$ preserves $\\sigma$ exactly. The first effect of an order-$k$ modification on <a href=\"#jets:fields\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:fields\">[jets:fields]</a> is $$\\label{jets:leading-action}\n \\Delta A_k=(1-k)br+hr',\\qquad \\Delta B_k=-khr.$$",
          "source_id": "PM-048.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "For $D$ as in <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> and $V=t^{\\ell+1}(AE_t+B\\partial_z)$ one has $$\\begin{aligned}\n=t^{k+\\ell+1}\\bigl(&((\\ell+1-k)bA+hA'-Bb')E_t\\notag\\\\\n &+((\\ell+1)bB+hB'-khA-Bh')\\partial_z\\bigr).\n \\label{jets:general-bracket}\n\\end{aligned}$$ Moreover, $$\\label{jets:divergence}\n \\mathcal L_D\\sigma=t^k\\bigl((k-4)b+h'\\bigr)\\sigma.$$ In particular, for $k\\ne4$ the choice $b=h'/(4-k)$ preserves $\\sigma$ exactly.       "exact_statement": "The order-two vertical obstruction vanishes.",

      "id": "C9",
      "kind": "theorem",
      "quantifiers": [],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-jets-second-obstruction"
    },
    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-jets",
        "exact_quote": "For the data of Propositions <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a> and <a href=\"#prop:residue-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:residue-data\">7</a>, there are finitely many holomorphic disk-to-outside transition germs, each a composition of flows <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> of orders $1,\\ldots,7$, together with holomorphic local rows $A_k,B_k$ for $0\\leq k\\leq7$, such that: the transitions fix $C$ and preserve its conormal bundle $L$; the vector fields <a href=\"#jets:fields\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:fields\">[jets:fields]</a> match through row index seven; and $A\\wedge B=t^2(1+O(t))$ on every chart.*\n\n*On any surface neighborhood $Y$ realizing these transition germs, write $\\mathcal I=\\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\\mathcal F=\\mathcal T_Y(-\\log C)$. The rows give two sections of $\\mathcal F/\\mathcal I^9\\mathcal F$, lying in $\\mathcal I\\mathcal F/\\mathcal I^9\\mathcal F$. Their determinant has leading term $t^4E_t\\wedge\\partial_z$, equivalently $t^5\\partial_t\\wedge\\partial_z$, with coefficient one.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-jets",
          "exact_quote": "For the data of Propositions <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a> and <a href=\"#prop:residue-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:residue-data\">7</a>, there are finitely many holomorphic disk-to-outside transition germs, each a composition of flows <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> of orders $1,\\ldots,7$, together with holomorphic local rows $A_k,B_k$ for $0\\leq k\\leq7$, such that: the transitions fix $C$ and preserve its conormal bundle $L$; the vector fields <a href=\"#jets:fields\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:fields\">[jets:fields]</a> match through row index seven; and $A\\wedge B=t^2(1+O(t))$ on every chart.*\n\n*On any surface neighborhood $Y$ realizing these transition germs, write $\\mathcal I=\\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\\mathcal F=\\mathcal T_Y(-\\log C)$. The rows give two sections of $\\mathcal F/\\mathcal I^9\\mathcal F$, lying in $\\mathcal I\\mathcal F/\\mathcal I^9\\mathcal F$. Their determinant has leading term $t^4E_t\\wedge\\partial_z$, equivalently $t^5\\partial_t\\wedge\\partial_z$, with coefficient one.",
          "source_id": "PM-048.1"

      "exact_statement": "For the data of Propositions <a href=\"#prop:curve-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:curve-data\">4</a> and <a href=\"#prop:residue-data\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:residue-data\">7</a>, there are finitely many holomorphic disk-to-outside transition germs, each a composition of flows <a href=\"#jets:generator\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:generator\">[jets:generator]</a> of orders $1,\\ldots,7$, together with holomorphic local rows $A_k,B_k$ for $0\\leq k\\leq7$, such that: the transitions fix $C$ and preserve its conormal bundle $L$; the vector fields <a href=\"#jets:fields\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"jets:fields\">[jets:fields]</a> match through row index seven; and $A\\wedge B=t^2(1+O(t))$ on every chart.*\n\n*On any surface neighborhood $Y$ realizing these transition germs, write $\\mathcal I=\\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\\mathcal F=\\mathcal T_Y(-\\log C)$.       "explanation": "A finite sequence of overlap flows yields compatible vector-field jets with the required leading determinant.",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-analytic-realization",
        "exact_quote": "The overlap maps of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:jets\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:jets\">10</a> define, after shrinking their domains, a smooth Hausdorff complex surface neighborhood $Y$ of $C$. Its conormal bundle is $L$, and hence $$C^2=-10.$$ Writing $\\mathcal I=\\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\\mathcal F=\\mathcal T_Y(-\\log C)$, the prescribed rows define two global sections $$\\bar v_1,\\bar v_2\\in H^0(Y,\\mathcal F/\\mathcal I^9\\mathcal F).$$ In divisor coordinates $(t,z)$, their local representatives have logarithmic determinant $t^4(1+O(t))$ and ordinary determinant $t^5(1+O(t))$.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-analytic-realization",
          "exact_quote": "The overlap maps of Proposition <a href=\"#prop:jets\" data-reference-type=\"ref\" data-reference=\"prop:jets\">10</a> define, after shrinking their domains, a smooth Hausdorff complex surface neighborhood $Y$ of $C$.           "source_id": "PM-048.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-analytic-graded-vanishing",

        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

          "source_id": "PM-048.1"
        }
      ],

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-analytic-counterexample",
        "exact_quote": "There is a normal complex surface germ $(X,x)$ with an isolated singular point and free analytic derivation sheaf of rank two: $$\\mathcal T_X=\\mathcal{H}om_{\\mathcal O_X}(\\Omega_X^1,\\mathcal O_X)\n \\simeq\\mathcal O_X^{\\oplus2}.$$ It has a resolution $\\pi:Y\\to X$ whose exceptional set is the smooth curve $C$ of genus $21$ and self-intersection $-10$.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-analytic-counterexample",
          "exact_quote": "There is a normal complex surface germ $(X,x)$ with an isolated singular point and free analytic derivation sheaf of rank two: $$\\mathcal T_X=\\mathcal{H}om_{\\mathcal O_X}(\\Omega_X^1,\\mathcal O_X)\n \\simeq\\mathcal O_X^{\\oplus2}.$$ It has a resolution $\\pi:Y\\to X$ whose exceptional set is the smooth curve $C$ of genus $21$ and self-intersection $-10$.",
          "source_id": "PM-048.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There is a normal complex surface germ $(X,x)$ with an isolated singular point and free analytic derivation sheaf of rank two: $$\\mathcal T_X=\\mathcal{H}om_{\\mathcal O_X}(\\Omega_X^1,\\mathcal O_X)\n \\simeq\\mathcal O_X^{\\oplus2}.$$ It has a resolution $\\pi:Y\\to X$ whose exceptional set is the smooth curve $C$ of genus $21$ and self-intersection $-10$.",
      "explanation": "Contracting the exceptional curve produces an isolated normal surface singularity with a free analytic derivation sheaf.",
      "fidelity": "PENDING",
      "hypotheses": [

          "exact_quote": "There is a normal complex surface germ $(X,x)$ with an isolated singular point and free analytic derivation sheaf of rank two:",

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-analytic-invariants",
        "exact_quote": "The construction also determines its canonical discrepancy and a lower bound for its geometric genus. The two-form $\\omega^\\circ$ dual to the frame $w_1,w_2$ generates the canonical sheaf on $X^\\circ$. Extending reflexively across the normal surface point gives $\\omega_X\\simeq\\mathcal O_X$; equivalently, we may choose the canonical divisor $K_X=0$. Since a normal surface is Cohen–Macaulay, this reflexive canonical sheaf is its dualizing sheaf, so $X$ is also Gorenstein; this surface criterion is recalled in [[FlennerZaidenberg2001](#ref-FlennerZaidenberg2001), 2.4, Definition 2.18 and the following paragraph]. By <a href=\"#analytic:ordinary-determinant\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"analytic:ordinary-determinant\">[analytic:ordinary-determinant]</a>, its pullback is $$\\pi^*\\omega^\\circ=t^{-5}u(t,z)^{-1}\\,dt\\wedge dz.$$ It is meromorphic on $Y$ with divisor exactly $-5C$. Thus $$K_Y=\\pi^*K_X-5C,$$ so the discrepancy of $C$ is $-5$.\n\nFor the geometric genus, put $\\mathcal O_{nC}=\\mathcal O_Y/\\mathcal I^n$. Riemann–Roch and the established equality $h^0(C,L)=2$ give $$h^1(C,L)=h^0(C,L^3)=12.$$ In the sequence $$0\\longrightarrow L\\longrightarrow\\mathcal O_{2C}\n \\longrightarrow\\mathcal O_C\\longrightarrow0,$$ the constant section of $\\mathcal O_C$ lifts to $\\mathcal O_{2C}$. Since $H^2(C,L)=0$, its cohomology sequence gives $$h^1(\\mathcal O_{2C})=h^1(C,L)+h^1(C,\\mathcal O_C)=12+21=33.$$ For every $n\\geq1$, the sequence with kernel $L^n$ similarly shows that $H^1(\\mathcal O_{(n+1)C})\\to H^1(\\mathcal O_{nC})$ is surjective. The theorem on formal functions, with the cofinal filtrations in <a href=\"#analytic:cofinal\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"analytic:cofinal\">[analytic:cofinal]</a>, identifies their inverse limit with the completion of $(R^1\\pi_*\\mathcal O_Y)_x$. This stalk has finite length, since the coherent higher direct image is supported at $x$, and thus equals its completion. The inverse limit maps onto $H^1(\\mathcal O_{2C})$, proving $$p_g(X,x)=\\dim_\\mathbb C(R^1\\pi_*\\mathcal O_Y)_x\\geq33.$$ The exceptional curve has genus $g=21$ and its dual graph has first Betti number $b=0$. Consequently $p_g-g-b\\geq12$.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

    {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy",
        "exact_quote": "Suppose that $T^1$ in <a href=\"#algebra:conormal-dual\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"algebra:conormal-dual\">[algebra:conormal-dual]</a> has finite length. There exist integers $N,n_0$ depending only on $f,D$ such that the following holds. If a row $g\\in P^s$ and an $s\\times t$ matrix $B$ satisfy $$gB=0,\\qquad\n g-f\\in\\mathfrak n^{n_0}P^s,\\qquad\n B-D\\in\\mathfrak n^NP^{s\\times t},$$ then there are a continuous $\\mathbb C$-algebra automorphism $\\Phi$ of $P$ and an invertible matrix $U\\in\\operatorname{GL}_s(P)$ such that $$f=\\Phi(g)U.$$ One can take $\\Phi(x_j)\\equiv x_j\\pmod{\\mathfrak n^2}$ and $U\\equiv1\\pmod{\\mathfrak n}$. Consequently $P/(g)\\simeq P/(f)$ as complete local $\\mathbb C$-algebras.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-algebraization",
        "exact_quote": "Let $(X,x)$ be a normal complex analytic germ of dimension $d\\ge1$, smooth away from $x$, and suppose that its holomorphic tangent module is free of rank $d$. There exist a finitely generated normal integral $\\mathbb C$-algebra $A$ and a maximal ideal $\\mathfrak p\\subset A$ such that $$\\dim A=d,\\qquad A/\\mathfrak p=\\mathbb C,\\qquad\n \\widehat{A_{\\mathfrak p}}\\simeq\\widehat{\\mathcal O_{X,x}},\\qquad\n \\mathop{\\mathrm{Der}}_{\\mathbb C}(A)\\simeq A^d.$$ The affine variety $\\mathop{\\mathrm{Spec}}A$ can be chosen smooth away from $\\mathfrak p$. If $x$ is singular, $A_{\\mathfrak p}$ is nonregular.",
        "source_id": "PM-048.1"
      },

<a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Algebraic derivation module** ([source](#pm-h-2)): For a finitely generated integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_A(\Omega^1_{A/\mathbb C},A).$$
- **Alternating product of coefficient rows** ([source](#pm-h-9)): For two rows $X=(X_1,X_2)$ and $Y=(Y_1,Y_2)$, put $$X\wedge Y=X_1Y_2-X_2Y_1.$$
- **Polar form** ([source](#pm-h-11)): For any quadratic form in this section, we use its polar form $$x\mathbin{\cdot}y=\frac{Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)}2, \qquad x^2=Q(x).$$
- **Flow generator** ([source](#jets:generator)): We modify the disk-to-outside identifications by flows of the vector fields $$\label{jets:generator} D=t^k(bE_t+h\partial_z),\qquad k>0,$$ where $b,h$ are holomorphic on the overlap in the disk coordinate.
- **Determinant target** ([source](#jets:target-equation)): Write the determinant equation as $$\label{jets:target-equation} A\wedge B=t^2G,\qquad G=1+G_1t+G_2t^2+\cdots.$$
- **Conormal relation kernel** ([source](#pm-h-31)): Define $$\psi:R^s\longrightarrow R^t,\qquad v\longmapsto v\bar D, \qquad K=\ker\psi, \qquad J=\sum_{j=1}^e R\,\overline{\partial_j f}.$$
- **Curve line bundles and divisor section** ([source](#pm-h-12)): Put $$\label{curve:line-bundles} H=F\|_C,\qquad L=H+Z,\qquad\theta=2L,$$ and let $\xi$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O_C(Z)$.
- **Ideal and logarithmic tangent sheaf** ([source](#pm-h-7)): Set $\mathcal I=\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\mathcal F=\mathcal T_Y(-\log C)$, the sheaf of vector fields tangent to $C$.
- **Meromorphic volume form** ([source](#pm-h-19)): The meromorphic two-form $$\label{jets:volume} \sigma=t^{-4}\frac{dt}{t}\wedge dz=t^{-5}dt\wedge dz$$ is invariant under these split changes of coordinates.
- **Trace quotient quadratic space** ([source](#stmt-trace-pairing)): The form on global sections has radical $\xi^2H^0(C,3H)$, and induces a nondegenerate symmetric form $\langle\ ,\ \rangle$ on $$\label{trace:W} W=H^0(C,\Lambda)/\xi^2H^0(C,3H),\qquad \dim W=2.$$
- **Conormal obstruction module** ([source](#pm-h-31)): Right-exact reduction of <a href="#algebra:ideal-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ideal-presentation">[algebra:ideal-presentation]</a> gives a presentation of $I/I^2$; its dual therefore identifies $$\label{algebra:conormal-dual} K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(I/I^2,R),\qquad T^1=K/J.$$

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R1** [claim](#stmt-curve-six-vectors) / [proof](#proof-stmt-curve-six-vectors): Quadratic coordinates and roots of a cubic supply paired normalized vectors. -> Normalized vectors avoiding the excluded directions.
- **R2** [claim](#stmt-prop-curve-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-curve-data): Contact on the quadric, adjunction and evaluation ranks determine the curve data. -> Canonical-root and interpolation data.
- **R3** [claim](#stmt-trace-principal-parts) / [proof](#proof-stmt-trace-principal-parts): Full principal parts are compared by residue duality and the local adjunction scalar. -> An isometry carrying evaluation directions to the trace quotient.
- **R4** [claim](#stmt-prop-residue-data) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-residue-data): Serre duality realizes pole prescriptions; the residue theorem gives the normalized wedge identities. -> Meromorphic sections with the required local and global residues.
- **R5** [claim](#stmt-prop-jets) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-jets): Overlap flows remove obstruction classes while compatible determinant targets are extended separately. -> Finite compatible vector-field jets with the prescribed determinant.
- **R6** [claim](#stmt-analytic-realization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-analytic-realization): Finite holomorphic overlap maps are glued through collars with a closed equivalence relation. -> A smooth Hausdorff neighborhood with the prescribed conormal bundle.
- **R7** [claim](#stmt-analytic-actual-lifting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-analytic-actual-lifting): Contraction, graded vanishing and formal functions give holomorphic lifts on a shrunken neighborhood. -> A holomorphic frame away from the exceptional curve.
- **R8** [claim](#stmt-prop-analytic-counterexample) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-analytic-counterexample): Adjunction proves singularity; Hartogs extension extends the frame as derivations. -> An isolated singular analytic germ with free tangent sheaf.
- **R9** [claim](#stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy) / [proof](#proof-stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy): Artin–Rees corrections improve equations while preserving relation precision; formal corrections converge. -> Formal equivalence for sufficiently close exact equation-and-relation solutions.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-prop-algebraization) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-algebraization): Artin approximation preserves the exact zero product; completion and faithful flatness descend freeness. -> A normal affine algebraization preserving the completed local ring.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-prop-algebraization-2): Apply algebraization to the analytic counterexample and shrink to an affine neighborhood. -> A singular normal affine complex surface with globally free derivations.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups) / [proof](#proof-stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups): The identity already has the universal property of the blowup at a free module. -> Derivation blowups and normalization preserve the singular surface.

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-leading**: The leading change of the field coefficients is determined by the generator and the leading row.
- **I-volume**: The generator preserves the meromorphic volume when its weighted divergence vanishes.
- **I-jets**: The finite transitions preserve the curve and its conormal bundle while matching the prescribed jets.
- **I-relations**: Approximation must retain an exact equation-and-relation zero product and sufficient closeness.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-smooth**: Treat a free tangent sheaf as a smoothness certificate. -> The constructed normal affine surface has a singular marked point despite tangent freeness.
- **A-truncation**: Algebraize by truncating the defining equations alone. -> Equation truncations need not retain the relations required for finite determinacy.
- **A-extension**: Require the dual volume form to extend holomorphically across the exceptional curve. -> The construction gives a pole along that curve.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The leading-action and volume-divergence calculation is finite and constructive. This implementation evaluates affine polynomial coefficients only; it does not construct the projective curve, solve the global cohomology problems, contract the curve, or algebraize the singularity.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON list of integer tuples [k,z,b0,b1,h0,h1,r0,r1].
# PRECONDITIONS: positive k; affine b,h,r; at most 1000 tuples.
# OUTPUTS: integer triples [Delta A_k,Delta B_k,divergence coefficient] at z.
# TIME: linear in tuple count, with exact integer operation costs.
# SPACE: linear in tuple count and integer bit length.
# ARITHMETIC: exact arbitrary precision integers using fmpz.
# FAILURE: invalid tuple shape, type, order or batch size raises ValueError.
import json,sys
from flint import fmpz
rows=json.load(sys.stdin)
if not isinstance(rows,list) or len(rows)>1000: raise ValueError('batch')
out=[]
for row in rows:
    if not isinstance(row,list) or len(row)!=8 or any(type(x) is not int for x in row) or row[0]<=0: raise ValueError('tuple')
    k,z,b0,b1,h0,h1,r0,r1=map(fmpz,row)
    b=b0+b1*z; h=h0+h1*z; r=r0+r1*z
    vertical=(1-k)*b*r+h*r1
    horizontal=-k*h*r
    divergence=(k-4)*b+h1
    out.append([int(vertical),int(horizontal),int(divergence)])
print(json.dumps(out,separators=(',',':')))
```

## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The construction establishes existence of a complex surface and does not supply a software implementation or a program contract. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The holomorphic neighborhood construction does not define a game model, a simulation state, or a numerical evolution contract. |
| tools | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The singular surface is a source of failure cases for symbolic geometry tools that infer regularity from a free derivation module. This tool use is inferred; the paper supplies no executable presentation of the affine algebra. |
| algorithms | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The explicit bracket and divergence identities suggest local symbolic checks of finite vector-field jets. This inferred use checks identities after coefficients are supplied; it does not compute the existential global construction. |
| data_structures | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Rows, relation matrices, and sheaves carry mathematical construction data; the paper does not specify storage operations or a data-structure contract. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The complex geometric construction supplies no hardware architecture, arithmetic circuit, or accelerator mapping. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The normal surface and its derivation module provide no cryptographic primitive or security assumption. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | The existence proof specifies no learned model, training objective, or statistical guarantee. |
| optimisation_operations_research | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Choices of sections and neighborhood shrinkings are geometric existence arguments, without an optimisation objective or an operations-research contract. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | Local overlap compatibility glues complex neighborhoods and does not specify a distributed protocol or a fault model. |
| other (algebraic geometry and singularity theory) | True | strong | inspirational | stated | The paper constructs a singular normal affine complex surface with free tangent sheaf and explains why derivation blowups fail to resolve its singular point. Its direct application is to geometric conjectures and the limits of regularity criteria. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **limit-1** (counterexample_scope): The construction and all germs and varieties are over the complex field; extension to other base fields is not established. Blocked use: ['Transferring the constructed surface to an arbitrary field without a separate argument.']
- **limit-2** (hypothesis): Algebraization assumes a normal complex analytic germ that is smooth away from the marked point and has a free holomorphic tangent module. Blocked use: ['Applying this algebraization statement to a nonnormal germ or a germ with an unverified nonsmooth locus.']
- **limit-3** (approximation): Finite determinacy requires finite length of the conormal obstruction module; the source does not give uniform numerical precision for arbitrary presentations. Blocked use: ['Choosing approximation precision without establishing the presentation-dependent bounds.']
- **limit-4** (hypothesis): The original relation matrix must generate all relations. The approximating matrix needs an exact zero product and sufficiently close approximation. Blocked use: ['Algebraizing by truncating defining equations without retaining their exact relations.']
- **limit-5** (formal_scope): Completed tangent freeness is expressed using continuous derivations and finite Jacobian presentations. Blocked use: ['Identifying ordinary algebraic differentials of a power-series ring with its continuous differentials.']
- **limit-6** (formal_scope): Finite compatible jets require separate analytic realization and holomorphic lifting before they give a frame on a punctured surface neighborhood. Blocked use: ['Treating a finite Taylor calculation as certification of the full singular affine surface construction.']
- **limit-7** (edge_case): The graded vanishing statement has a specified range; the earlier gluing orders require their own obstruction arguments. Blocked use: ['Using the graded vanishing statement below its stated range.']
- **limit-8** (edge_case): At the exceptional transition order, determinant-target compatibility uses an exact differential and a residue argument. Blocked use: ['Applying the volume-preserving formula with a vanishing denominator.']
- **limit-9** (nonconstructive): The curve construction selects a general member of an affine parameter space and avoids proper affine hyperplanes. An explicit defining equation is not supplied by this selection argument. Blocked use: ['Claiming that the existential selection is an executable algorithm producing a concrete surface equation.']
- **limit-10** (counterexample_scope): The geometric genus is bounded below rather than computed exactly. Blocked use: ['Replacing the stated lower bound by an exact geometric genus.']
- **limit-11** (counterexample_scope): No tangent frame near the singular point can be chosen commuting. Blocked use: ['Treating a free tangent module as a commuting coordinate frame.']
- **limit-12** (counterexample_scope): Iteration of derivation-module blowups, including normalization after each step, leaves the constructed singular surface unchanged. Blocked use: ['Using this procedure to resolve the constructed singular point.']
- **limit-13** (counterexample_scope): The surface is affine and singular; the compact complex surface classification cited in the source has a different scope. Blocked use: ['Transferring the affine example into a compact complex surface with free tangent sheaf.']

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We construct a singular normal affine complex surface whose tangent sheaf is free of rank two. This disproves the Lipman–Zariski conjecture in characteristic zero.

<a id="pm-h-1"></a>
#### Statements index

| anchor | kind | label | number | first sentence |

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

For a finitely generated integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$, write $$\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_A(\Omega^1_{A/\mathbb C},A).$$ The module $\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)$ consists of the algebraic vector fields on $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$. On a smooth variety of dimension $d$, the tangent sheaf is locally free of rank $d$. The Lipman–Zariski conjecture asks for the converse: a complex algebraic variety with locally free tangent sheaf should be nonsingular. In affine terms, it asserts that local freeness of $\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)$ forces $A$ to be regular. We prove the following.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exist a finitely generated normal integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$ of Krull dimension two and a maximal ideal $\mathfrak m\subset A$ such that $$\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^{\oplus2},
\qquad A_{\mathfrak m}\text{ is not regular}.$$*

</div>

Here freeness holds on the entire affine surface, which can be chosen smooth away from the marked point. We first construct an isolated Gorenstein analytic surface singularity. The passage to algebraic geometry preserves its completed local ring, from which tangent freeness descends.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Context and significance

Lipman’s study of free derivation modules established that local freeness of the tangent sheaf forces normality <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Lipman1965](#ref-Lipman1965), Theorem 3]. The conjecture thus asks whether the same hypothesis excludes the remaining normal singularities. In positive characteristic the corresponding assertion fails even for hypersurfaces: over a perfect field of characteristic $p$, the singular surface $xy-z^p=0$ has tangent sheaf freely generated by $x\partial_x-y\partial_y$ and $\partial_z$ <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[GrafPositive2022](#ref-GrafPositive2022), 1]. The construction here has a different source of tangent freeness and takes place entirely over $\mathbb C$.

Several positive results explain both the strength of tangent freeness and the role of surfaces in the problem. Hochster proved that a reduced finitely generated characteristic-zero algebra with a nonnegative grading and degree-zero part equal to the base field is a polynomial algebra if its derivation module is free at the irrelevant maximal ideal <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Hochster1977](#ref-Hochster1977), 1, Theorem]. Van Straten and Steenbrink used extension of differential forms to prove the isolated-singularity case in dimension at least three <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[VanStratenSteenbrink1985](#ref-VanStratenSteenbrink1985), Theorem 1.3 and 1.6]. Their extension argument leaves the surface case as a distinct problem.

Hypersurfaces were treated by Scheja and Storch <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[SchejaStorch1972](#ref-SchejaStorch1972), Satz 9.3]; Källström established the characteristic-zero algebraic locally complete-intersection case <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Kallstrom2011](#ref-Kallstrom2011), Corollary 1.3(1), corrected version]. Extension theorems for differential forms also give strong results defined by the singularities of the canonical divisor. Greb, Kebekus, Kovács and Peternell proved the conjecture for Kawamata log terminal spaces <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[GKKP2011](#ref-GKKP2011), Theorem 6.1]. The log-canonical case was proved by Druel <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[Druel2014](#ref-Druel2014), Theorem 1.1] and by Graf and Kovács <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[GrafKovacs2014](#ref-GrafKovacs2014), Corollary 1.3]. These results relate tangent freeness to the behavior of differential forms on a resolution. Another distinction concerns the Lie bracket: Jörder proved smoothness for a normal complex space whose tangent sheaf locally admits a basis of pairwise commuting vector fields <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Jorder2014](#ref-Jorder2014), Theorem 1.1]. Thus no tangent frame near our singular point can be chosen commuting.

For normal complex surfaces, the cohomology and configuration of the exceptional curves give further criteria. Graf proved smoothness when the geometric genus $p_g$ is at most one, and also when $p_g=2$ and the exceptional divisor is not a tree of rational curves <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[GrafLowGenus2021](#ref-GrafLowGenus2021), Theorem 1.1]. Bergner and Graf then obtained the criterion $$p_g-g-b\le2,$$ where $g$ is the sum of the genera of the exceptional curves and $b$ is the first Betti number of the resolution’s dual graph <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[BergnerGraf2020](#ref-BergnerGraf2020), Theorem 1]. The analytic singularity constructed here has one exceptional curve of genus $21$ and self-intersection $-10$. Its canonical discrepancy is $-5$, and $p_g\ge33$, so $p_g-g-b\ge12$. Remark <a href="#analytic:invariants" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:invariants">15</a> proves these calculations. They place the analytic example beyond the log-canonical and geometric-genus criteria just described.

Global hypotheses provide other positive cases. Biswas, Gurjar and Kolte prove smoothness for a normal affine surface whose tangent bundle on the smooth locus is trivial and whose smooth locus has logarithmic Kodaira dimension at most one <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[BiswasGurjarKolte2014](#ref-BiswasGurjarKolte2014), Theorem 0.1(1)]. Consequently, the smooth locus $U$ of the affine surface in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has logarithmic Kodaira dimension two (Corollary <a href="#algebra:log-kodaira" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:log-kodaira">18</a>). Here logarithmic Kodaira dimension means the Iitaka dimension of $K_{\overline U}+D$ on a smooth projective compactification with reduced simple-normal-crossing boundary $D$. This global consequence uses the algebraic endpoint of the construction, not just the analytic germ. In contrast, Graf’s compact-surface result identifies compact complex surfaces with free rank-two tangent sheaf as complex two-tori <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[GrafLowGenus2021](#ref-GrafLowGenus2021), Corollary 1.3].

The example also gives a negative answer in characteristic zero to the derivation-blowup question of Barajas, Chávez-Martínez and Romano-Velázquez <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[BarajasChavezRomano2026](#ref-BarajasChavezRomano2026), Introduction, Question 1]. The blowup at a module is the universal proper birational modification making its pullback modulo torsion locally free. As those authors observe, a singular variety with free tangent sheaf cannot be resolved by iterating this operation at the module of derivations: every step is the identity. For our normal surface, inserting normalization does not change this conclusion (Corollary <a href="#algebra:derivation-blowups" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:derivation-blowups">19</a>).

The neighborhood of the exceptional curve is essential to the argument. Hochster’s theorem excludes obtaining the example from a singular positively graded cone with free tangent sheaf. Instead, we start with a split line bundle and change the gluing maps of its zero-section neighborhood while preserving the normal bundle. This freedom supplies the required vector fields. Their dual volume form has a fifth-order pole along the exceptional curve, so holomorphic extension across that curve is not an input to the construction.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Main ideas and proof structure

The construction first produces an analytic surface singularity and then passes to an affine algebraic surface. The analytic step requires two vector fields on a punctured neighborhood that remain linearly independent everywhere. We build those fields on a resolution, where the exceptional set is a smooth negative curve. Their determinant vanishes along that curve to a prescribed order, and nowhere else near it.

Let $C$ be a smooth curve and $L$ a positive line bundle with $L^{\otimes4}\simeq K_C$. Its dual bundle $L^{-1}$ provides the initial neighborhood of $C$. In compatible base and fiber coordinates $(z,t)$, that neighborhood carries the meromorphic two-form $$\sigma=t^{-5}\,dt\wedge dz.$$ We seek two vector fields of the form $$v_i=t\bigl(A_i(t,z)t\partial_t+B_i(t,z)\partial_z\bigr),
\qquad i=1,2,$$ with $\sigma(v_1,v_2)=1+O(t)$. The leading vertical coefficients form a pair $r=(r_1,r_2)$ of sections of $L$ with six simple common zeros, whose reduced divisor is denoted by $Z$. These common zeros create the main compatibility problem: each requires a pointwise correction to make the leading determinant coefficient nonzero along $C$, but the corrections must also satisfy global residue constraints to glue the vector fields. The curve and trace construction produces data satisfying both requirements.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
###### A curve and normalized residues.

Section <a href="#curve:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:section">2</a> constructs $C$ on $\mathbb P^1\times\mathbb P^1$, with fourth-order contact at $Z$ with an auxiliary elliptic curve $\Gamma$. A two-torsion relation on $\Gamma$ controls the section dimensions on $C$ and supplies six vectors in a two-dimensional quadratic space. The resulting curve has genus $21$, and $\deg L=10$.

Section <a href="#trace:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="trace:section">3</a> transports these vectors to $C$ by a trace pairing for a degree-four map $C\to\mathbb P^1$. Residue duality compares principal parts on the two curves and fixes the scalar and sign of the pairing. Proposition <a href="#prop:residue-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:residue-data">7</a> then gives a meromorphic pair $P$ of sections of $L^2$ whose local expansions enforce the determinant condition and whose residue sums cancel the gluing obstructions.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Finite vector-field jets.

In Section <a href="#jets:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jets:section">4</a>, we modify the neighborhood by finitely many holomorphic flows and choose compatible coefficients of the fields. At order two the obstruction separates into a symmetric residue sum and one antisymmetric sum; the normalized residue data cancel both. Thereafter, we extend the vector fields and their desired determinant as separate but compatible finite Taylor expansions. Choosing the determinant coefficients before solving for the field coefficients leaves the freedom needed to remove the remaining obstructions. Exactness of a differential and cohomology vanishing finish the finite induction. Proposition <a href="#prop:jets" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:jets">10</a> supplies finite jets with determinant $t^5(1+O(t))$ in ordinary coordinates, to sufficient order for the holomorphic lifting below.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
###### Holomorphic lifting and contraction.

Section <a href="#analytic:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="analytic:section">5</a> realizes the finite transition maps as a Hausdorff surface neighborhood $Y$ of $C$. Set $\mathcal I=\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\mathcal F=\mathcal T_Y(-\log C)$, the sheaf of vector fields tangent to $C$. Grauert’s criterion contracts the curve to a point $x$ of a normal surface $X$ <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[Grauert1962](#ref-Grauert1962)]. Cohomology of the successive ideal quotients and the theorem on formal functions <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[Grauert1960](#ref-Grauert1960)] give the cohomological vanishing needed to lift these jets to holomorphic fields after shrinking, without requiring an infinite sequence of neighborhood changes to converge. Their determinant gives a frame on $Y\setminus C\simeq X\setminus\{x\}$, which normality extends to a free tangent sheaf on $X$. Adjunction proves that $x$ is singular.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
###### The affine algebraic endpoint.

Section <a href="#algebra:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:section">6</a> combines approximation with the finite determinacy of equations together with their relations. The latter method appears in Hironaka’s equivalence theorem <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Hironaka1969](#ref-Hironaka1969), 3, Theorem 3.3] and in Artin’s algebraization of isolated singularities <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[Artin1969](#ref-Artin1969), Theorems 1.10 and 3.8–3.9]. We prove the required finite-determinacy statement with a relation matrix: the approximated equations and matrix retain their exact zero product. This produces an algebraic local ring with the same completion as $\mathcal O_{X,x}$. A finite Jacobian presentation identifies its completed derivation module with the continuous derivations of that completion. Faithful flatness descends freeness and the local ring properties. Finally, shrinking to one affine neighborhood gives the globally free module in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, while preserving the singular point.

<div id="tab:notation">

| Notation                        | Meaning and normalization                                                                                                                               |
|:--------------------------------|:--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| $C$, $Z$                        | Smooth genus-$21$ curve and its six marked points.                                                                                                      |
| $L$, $r$                        | $\deg L=10$, $L^4\simeq K_C$; the two sections in $r$ have common zero divisor $Z$.                                                                     |
| $\Gamma$, $P$                   | Auxiliary elliptic curve; meromorphic pair of sections of $L^2$ supplying the residue identities.                                                       |
| $Y$, $\mathcal I$, $\mathcal F$ | Surface neighborhood of $C$; $\mathcal I=\mathcal O_Y(-C)$ and $\mathcal F=\mathcal T_Y(-\log C)$. The jets lie in $\mathcal F/\mathcal I^9\mathcal F$. |
| $\pi:Y\to X$                    | Contraction of $C$ to the singular point $x$; $C^2=-10$.                                                                                                |
| $A$, $\mathfrak p$              | Affine algebra and marked singular point; $\widehat{A_{\mathfrak p}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal O_{X,x}}$.                                                  |

The main objects in the construction, in their order of use.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Conventions

Line bundles on curves are written additively when convenient: $aL+bH$ means $L^{\otimes a}\otimes H^{\otimes b}$, and $H^q(L)$ means $H^q(C,L)$. A divisor and its associated line bundle may share a symbol. For two rows $X=(X_1,X_2)$ and $Y=(Y_1,Y_2)$, put $$X\wedge Y=X_1Y_2-X_2Y_1.$$ The symmetric bilinear form associated with a quadratic form $Q$ is normalized by $\langle x,x\rangle=Q(x)$. A residue on an annular overlap means the contour integral divided by $2\pi i$. Logarithmic residue and adjunction use the convention that the normal differential comes first. All germs and varieties are over $\mathbb C$. We use standard facts about algebraic curve cohomology and adjunction as in <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[Hartshorne1977](#ref-Hartshorne1977)], and about coherent analytic sheaves and normal spaces as in <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[GrauertRemmert1984](#ref-GrauertRemmert1984)].

<a id="curve:section"></a>
### A curve with prescribed residue data

We use additive notation for line bundles. If $B$ is a line bundle and $D$ is a divisor, $B+D$ means $B\otimes\mathcal O(D)$; restrictions of bundles will occasionally be left implicit. We first construct a marked curve and a fourth root of its canonical bundle. An auxiliary elliptic curve will control the directions and the normalization of the residue data. The output is collected in Proposition <a href="#prop:curve-data" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:curve-data">4</a>: besides $L^4\simeq K_C$, we need precise section dimensions and evaluation maps at six marked points. These conditions will make the low-order gluing obstructions computable.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### The elliptic model and its quadratic form

Let $\Gamma$ initially be a smooth elliptic curve. Choose a degree-two line bundle $F$ and a line bundle $D_0$ of exact order four, and set $S=F+D_0$. Both $F$ and $S$ are generated by their two-dimensional spaces of sections. The product of their associated maps embeds $\Gamma$ in $$\Sigma=\mathbb P^1\times\mathbb P^1.$$ Indeed, after choosing an origin on $\Gamma$, the two fiber involutions have the form $p\mapsto a-p$ and $p\mapsto a+d_0-p$, where $d_0\ne0$ represents $D_0$. Two distinct points cannot be interchanged by both involutions, and a point cannot be fixed by both. The product map is therefore injective and immersive, hence is a closed embedding. Its two projection degrees show that its image has bidegree $(2,2)$. We now also write $F=(1,0)$ and $S=(0,1)$ for the ruling bundles on $\Sigma$.

<a id="curve:bundle-identities"></a>
On $\Gamma$ put $$\Delta=2D_0,\qquad \Phi=F+\Delta,\qquad M=3\Phi.$$ Thus $\Delta$ is nontrivial of order two, and $$\Gamma|_\Gamma=2F+2S=4F+\Delta,
  \qquad M=3F+\Delta,\qquad 2M=6F.$$ Consequently $$\label{curve:bundle-identities}
\begin{split}
  4M&=(2\Gamma+4F)|_\Gamma,\\
  2M-(\Gamma+F)|_\Gamma&=\Phi,\\
  (\Gamma+2F)|_\Gamma-2M&=\Delta.
\end{split}$$ Fix isomorphisms in these identities compatibly. In particular, viewing $\Phi$ as $2M-(\Gamma+F)|_\Gamma$, the first identity induces the identification $2\Phi=2F|_\Gamma$ that we use below.

Since $K_\Sigma+\Gamma=0$, choose a nowhere vanishing logarithmic two-form $\Omega$ with pole divisor $\Gamma$. Its residue $\omega=\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_\Gamma\Omega$ is a nowhere vanishing differential on $\Gamma$. Our residue convention places the normal differential first: locally $\mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_{y=0}((dy/y)\wedge dz)=dz$. Choose a basis $u=(u_1,u_2)$ of $H^0(\Sigma,F)$, and write $$\eta_\Gamma=u_1\,du_2-u_2\,du_1
       \in H^0(\Gamma,K_\Gamma+2F).$$ The expression is independent of the local frame for $F$: in that frame it is the indicated coefficient differential times the square of the frame.

<a id="curve:quadratic"></a>
There is a unique element of $\mathop{\mathrm{Sym}}^2H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)$ whose image under multiplication is $$\label{curve:quadratic}
  -\frac{\eta_\Gamma}{\omega}\in H^0(\Gamma,2\Phi)
       =H^0(\Gamma,2F).$$ It defines a nondegenerate quadratic form $Q$ on $H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)^*$. To see both existence and nondegeneracy, let $\iota_F$ and $\iota_\Phi$ be the two degree-two involutions. One has $\iota_\Phi=t_\Delta\iota_F$, where $t_\Delta$ is translation by the nonzero point of order two corresponding to $\Delta$. The four ramification points of $F|_\Gamma$, namely the fixed points of $\iota_F$, are paired without fixed points by $\iota_\Phi$. They form two distinct reduced fibers of the $\Phi$-map. Their divisor is therefore the pullback of the zero divisor of a binary quadratic with two distinct roots. Scaling this quadratic gives exactly <a href="#curve:quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:quadratic">[curve:quadratic]</a>. Uniqueness follows because pullback from the surjective $\Phi$-map is injective on sections of $\mathcal O_{\mathbb P^1}(2)$.

For $q\in\Gamma$, evaluation in a local frame of $\Phi$ gives a nonzero element of $H^0(\Gamma,\Phi)^*$, defined up to a nonzero scalar. The corresponding projective direction will be denoted by $[\operatorname{ev}_q]$. By <a href="#curve:quadratic" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:quadratic">[curve:quadratic]</a>, this direction is isotropic precisely when $q$ is a ramification point of $F|_\Gamma$. For any quadratic form in this section, we use its polar form $$x\mathbin{\cdot}y=\frac{Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)}2,
 \qquad x^2=Q(x).$$

The six vectors below encode both a pointwise normalization and a total residue. If $w=\sum_jw_j$ and $w_j^2+w_j\mathbin{\cdot}w=1$ at each marked point, then $$\sum_{j=1}^6(w_j^2-1)=-w^2.$$ The curve we construct has $\deg L=10$. Thus the choice $w^2=90$ will give the total $-9\deg L$ required by the order-two obstruction in Section <a href="#jets:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="jets:section">4</a>, while retaining all six pointwise normalizations.

<a id="stmt-curve-six-vectors"></a>
<div id="curve:six-vectors" class="lemma">

<a id="curve:vector-conditions"></a>
**Lemma 2**. *In a two-dimensional nondegenerate complex quadratic space, one can choose three distinct nonisotropic directions, avoiding any prescribed finite set of projective directions, and nonzero vectors $w_1,\ldots,w_6$ that use each of these directions twice, with the two vectors in each pair equal, such that $$\label{curve:vector-conditions}
  w=\sum_{j=1}^6w_j,\qquad
  w^2=90,\qquad w_j^2+w_j\mathbin{\cdot}w=1
  \quad(1\le j\le6).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curve-six-vectors"><summary>Proof of stmt-curve-six-vectors</summary>

*Proof.* Choose coordinates in which $Q(x,y)=xy$, so that $(x,y)\mathbin{\cdot}(x',y')=(xy'+yx')/2$. Set $$h=\sqrt{90}>0,\quad R=\sqrt{1+h^2/4}>0,\quad
 b=\frac{2h}{R},\quad
 d(z)=\left(Rz-\frac h2,\frac Rz-\frac h2\right).$$ Direct expansion gives $$d(z)^2+d(z)\mathbin{\cdot}(h,h)=R^2-h^2/4=1.$$ For a sufficiently small general nonzero complex number $\lambda$, let $z_1,z_2,z_3$ be the roots of $$X^3-bX^2+b\lambda X-\lambda=0.$$ Vieta’s formulas give $$\sum_{i=1}^3z_i=b,\qquad
 \sum_{i=1}^3z_i^{-1}=b,\qquad
 2\sum_{i=1}^3d(z_i)=(h,h).$$ Thus taking each $d(z_i)$ twice gives <a href="#curve:vector-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:vector-conditions">[curve:vector-conditions]</a>.

For completeness, these vectors have the required distinct directions. As $\lambda\to0$, two roots approach zero and the third approaches $b$. The roots are distinct for general $\lambda$: the cubic discriminant has nonzero leading term $-4b^3\lambda$ at zero. Near zero the projective ratio of the two entries of $d(z)$ is $$\frac{z(Rz-h/2)}{R-hz/2},$$ whose derivative at zero is $-h/(2R)\ne0$. The two small roots therefore give distinct nonisotropic directions. At $z=b$ the entries are $3h/2$ and $(R^2-h^2)/(2h)$, both nonzero, so the third limiting direction is different from their common limit. These properties persist for sufficiently small general $\lambda$. Finally apply simultaneously to all six vectors an isometry $(x,y)\mapsto(\nu x,\nu^{-1}y)$. On nonisotropic projective directions this multiplies the ratio by $\nu^2$. Only finitely many values of $\nu\in\mathbb C^*$ are excluded by the prescribed finite set, and all equations in <a href="#curve:vector-conditions" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:vector-conditions">[curve:vector-conditions]</a> are preserved. ◻

</details>

<a id="curve:marked-divisor"></a>
Apply Lemma <a href="#curve:six-vectors" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="curve:six-vectors">2</a> to the quadratic form $Q$, excluding the branch values of the $\Phi$-map. Each of its three directions is then $[\operatorname{ev}_q]$ at two distinct points of $\Gamma$. Label these points $q_1,\ldots,q_6$ so that $[w_j]=[\operatorname{ev}_{q_j}]$, and put $$\label{curve:marked-divisor}
 Z=q_1+\cdots+q_6\in|3\Phi|=|M|.$$ Choose a section $\beta$ of $M$ with divisor $Z$. Nonisotropy implies that $F|_\Gamma$ is unramified at all six points. Their $F$-values are not all equal, since a degree-two fiber cannot contain six distinct points.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### Fourth-order contact on the quadric

We have chosen the six points and their quadratic-space data on the elliptic curve. We now construct the curve $C$ through those points. Fourth-order contact will transfer the short evaluation schemes from $C$ to $\Gamma$, where their section dimensions are easy to compute.

Write $\gamma$ for the section of $\mathcal O_\Sigma(\Gamma)$ defining $\Gamma$. We choose a section $$\kappa\in H^0(\Sigma,2\Gamma+4F),\qquad
 \kappa|_\Gamma=\beta^4,$$ using the fixed identification in <a href="#curve:bundle-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:bundle-identities">[curve:bundle-identities]</a>. Such sections exist: restriction to $\Gamma$ has kernel $\mathcal O_\Sigma(\Gamma+4F)=\mathcal O_\Sigma(6,2)$, whose first cohomology vanishes. The affine space of choices is $$\kappa_0+\gamma H^0(\Sigma,\Gamma+4F).$$ A general choice defines a smooth curve $C$. Indeed, the associated linear system is without base points off $\Gamma$, so Bertini’s Theorem gives smoothness there. On $\Gamma\setminus Z$ every choice is nonzero. At each $q_j$, global generation of $\mathcal O_\Sigma(6,2)$ allows the normal derivative to vary nontrivially. Avoiding six proper affine hyperplanes makes all those derivatives nonzero. Their complements and the Bertini open meet in the same irreducible affine parameter space; independence of the six evaluation functionals is unnecessary.

<a id="curve:contact-expansion"></a>
More explicitly, near $q_j$ choose a local equation $y=0$ for $\Gamma$, a coordinate $z$ on $\Gamma$ vanishing at $q_j$, and frames in which $\kappa$ has coefficient $k(y,z)$ and $\beta$ has coefficient $b(z)$. The frames may be chosen compatibly with <a href="#curve:bundle-identities" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:bundle-identities">[curve:bundle-identities]</a>, so that $$k(0,z)=b(z)^4,\qquad b'(0)\ne0,\qquad k_y(0,0)\ne0.$$ The implicit function theorem expresses $C$ as $y=\varphi(z)$, where $$\label{curve:contact-expansion}
 \varphi(z)=-\frac{b'(0)^4}{k_y(0,0)}z^4+O(z^5).$$ Thus the contact order is exactly four and $\operatorname{div}_C(\gamma|_C)=4Z$, with the same six marked points now regarded as points of $C$.

<a id="curve:canonical-root"></a>
<a id="curve:adjunction-normalization"></a>
The bidegree of $C$ is $(8,4)$. The sequence for its defining divisor, together with $H^1(\Sigma,\mathcal O_\Sigma(-8,-4))=0$, gives $H^0(C,\mathcal O_C)=\mathbb C$. Hence the smooth curve $C$ is connected and irreducible. Put <a id="curve:line-bundles"></a>$$\label{curve:line-bundles}
 H=F|_C,\qquad L=H+Z,\qquad\theta=2L,$$ and let $\xi$ be the canonical section of $\mathcal O_C(Z)$. The divisor equality above specifies the isomorphism $4\mathcal O_C(Z)\simeq\mathcal O_C(\Gamma)$ by $\xi^4\mapsto\gamma|_C$. Together with adjunction, this gives $$\label{curve:canonical-root}
 4L=4H+4Z\simeq(\Gamma+4F)|_C\simeq K_C.$$ We fix the last isomorphism by the following normalization. For a local section $s$ of $\Gamma+4F$, it is $$\label{curve:adjunction-normalization}
 s|_C\longmapsto
 \mathop{\mathrm{Res}}_C\left(\frac{s\gamma\Omega}{\kappa}\right),$$ with the normal differential first. This formula fixes the scalar in <a href="#curve:canonical-root" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:canonical-root">[curve:canonical-root]</a> for the later residue computations.

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Section counts and evaluation maps

We give the jet calculation underlying all the special-divisor dimensions. The section spaces below are algebraic section spaces; equivalently, they are holomorphic section spaces on these projective curves, by the comparison theorem of Serre <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[SerreGAGA1956](#ref-SerreGAGA1956), Theorem 1].

<a id="stmt-curve-adjoint-jets"></a>
<div id="curve:adjoint-jets" class="lemma">

<a id="curve:residual-table"></a>
**Lemma 3**. *For $0\le j\le3$, set $$\mathcal A_j=\Gamma+(4-j)F=(6-j,2),\qquad
 N_j=\mathcal A_j|_\Gamma=(8-j)F+\Delta.$$ Restriction identifies $H^0(\Sigma,\mathcal A_j)$ with $H^0(C,K_C-jH)$ and maps it onto $H^0(\Gamma,N_j)$. For $1\le\ell\le4$, evaluation on the length-$\ell$ subschemes at the six marked points has the same rank on $C$ as on $\Gamma$. The ranks needed here are $$\label{curve:residual-table}
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c|c|c}
 j&\ell& h^0(\Gamma,N_j)&N_j-\ell Z
   &h^0(\Gamma,N_j-\ell Z)&\text{evaluation rank}\\ \hline
 0&1&16&5F&10&6\\
 1&2&14&\Phi&2&12\\
 2&2&12&\Delta&0&12\\
 3&2&10&-F+\Delta&0&10
\end{array}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-curve-adjoint-jets"><summary>Proof of stmt-curve-adjoint-jets</summary>

*Proof.* The restriction kernels on $\Sigma$ are respectively $$\mathcal A_j-C=(-2-j,-2),\qquad
 \mathcal A_j-\Gamma=(4-j,0).$$ Product cohomology on $\mathbb P^1\times\mathbb P^1$ gives $H^0=H^1=0$ for the first kernel and $H^1=0$ for the second. Adjunction therefore gives the two assertions about restriction.

By <a href="#curve:contact-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="curve:contact-expansion">[curve:contact-expansion]</a>, the ideals of the two length-$\ell$ subschemes in the local surface are $$(y-\varphi(z),z^\ell)=(y,z^\ell)\qquad(\ell\le4).$$ Thus these are the same embedded subscheme, and restrictions of the surface line bundle and its sections to them agree. The two surjectivity statements just proved imply equality of evaluation ranks.

On the elliptic curve, $\deg F=2$, $Z=3F+\Delta$, and $2Z=6F$.
