If $f$ is any local defining equation of the embedding, then $$\sum_{\alpha=1}^e
 a_{i\alpha}\frac{\partial f}{\partial x_\alpha}=0$$ on $X^\circ$ and hence on $X$, by density and reducedness. The tuple $(a_{i\alpha})_\alpha$ consequently defines a section of $\mathcal{H}om_{\mathcal O_X}(\Omega_X^1,\mathcal O_X)$ extending $w_i$. We continue to denote that extension by $w_i$.

For any local analytic derivation $w$ on $X$, the frame on $X^\circ$ gives unique holomorphic coefficients $h_1,h_2$ there such that $w=h_1w_1+h_2w_2$. Both coefficients extend by <a href="#analytic:hartogs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:hartogs">[analytic:hartogs]</a>. The extended equality holds on $X$, since applying its difference to the embedding coordinates gives holomorphic functions vanishing on the dense open set $X^\circ$. This proves surjectivity of the map $$\mathcal O_X^{\oplus2}\longrightarrow\mathcal T_X,
 \qquad(h_1,h_2)\longmapsto h_1w_1+h_2w_2.$$ If a pair lies in its kernel, both coefficients vanish on $X^\circ$ because $w_1,w_2$ are a frame there, and therefore vanish on $X$. The map is also injective, proving the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-analytic-invariants"></a>
<div id="analytic:invariants" class="remark">

*Remark 15*. The construction also determines its canonical discrepancy and a lower bound for its geometric genus. The two-form $\omega^\circ$ dual to the frame $w_1,w_2$ generates the canonical sheaf on $X^\circ$. Extending reflexively across the normal surface point gives $\omega_X\simeq\mathcal O_X$; equivalently, we may choose the canonical divisor $K_X=0$. Since a normal surface is Cohen–Macaulay, this reflexive canonical sheaf is its dualizing sheaf, so $X$ is also Gorenstein; this surface criterion is recalled in <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[FlennerZaidenberg2001](#ref-FlennerZaidenberg2001), 2.4, Definition 2.18 and the following paragraph]. By <a href="#analytic:ordinary-determinant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:ordinary-determinant">[analytic:ordinary-determinant]</a>, its pullback is $$\pi^*\omega^\circ=t^{-5}u(t,z)^{-1}\,dt\wedge dz.$$ It is meromorphic on $Y$ with divisor exactly $-5C$. Thus $$K_Y=\pi^*K_X-5C,$$ so the discrepancy of $C$ is $-5$.

For the geometric genus, put $\mathcal O_{nC}=\mathcal O_Y/\mathcal I^n$. Riemann–Roch and the established equality $h^0(C,L)=2$ give $$h^1(C,L)=h^0(C,L^3)=12.$$ In the sequence $$0\longrightarrow L\longrightarrow\mathcal O_{2C}
 \longrightarrow\mathcal O_C\longrightarrow0,$$ the constant section of $\mathcal O_C$ lifts to $\mathcal O_{2C}$. Since $H^2(C,L)=0$, its cohomology sequence gives $$h^1(\mathcal O_{2C})=h^1(C,L)+h^1(C,\mathcal O_C)=12+21=33.$$ For every $n\geq1$, the sequence with kernel $L^n$ similarly shows that $H^1(\mathcal O_{(n+1)C})\to H^1(\mathcal O_{nC})$ is surjective. The theorem on formal functions, with the cofinal filtrations in <a href="#analytic:cofinal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="analytic:cofinal">[analytic:cofinal]</a>, identifies their inverse limit with the completion of $(R^1\pi_*\mathcal O_Y)_x$. This stalk has finite length, since the coherent higher direct image is supported at $x$, and thus equals its completion. The inverse limit maps onto $H^1(\mathcal O_{2C})$, proving $$p_g(X,x)=\dim_\mathbb C(R^1\pi_*\mathcal O_Y)_x\geq33.$$ The exceptional curve has genus $g=21$ and its dual graph has first Betti number $b=0$. Consequently $p_g-g-b\geq12$.

</div>

<a id="algebra:section"></a>
### Algebraization and the affine counterexample

We now pass from the analytic germ to an affine variety. Approximating the defining equations does not by itself retain their relations. We approximate the equations and a finite relation matrix together; their exact zero product, together with sufficiently close approximation, will recover the same complete local ring. Tangent freeness then descends from the continuous derivations of that ring. This follows the equation-and-relation determinacy method of Hironaka <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Hironaka1969](#ref-Hironaka1969), 3, Theorem 3.3], used in Artin’s isolated-singularity algebraization <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[Artin1969](#ref-Artin1969), Theorems 3.8–3.9]. We prove the finite-determinacy statement needed here, including fixed estimates that avoid a complete-intersection hypothesis.

<a id="pm-h-31"></a>
#### Finite determinacy of equations with relations

<a id="algebra:ideal-presentation"></a>
<a id="algebra:conormal-dual"></a>
Put $$P=\mathbb C[[x_1,\ldots,x_e]],\qquad
 \mathfrak n=(x_1,\ldots,x_e),\qquad
 I=(f_1,\ldots,f_s),\qquad R=P/I,$$ where $f=(f_1,\ldots,f_s)\in\mathfrak nP^s$ is a row. Write $\mathfrak m_R=\mathfrak nR$, and let an overbar denote reduction modulo $I$. Choose an $s\times t$ matrix $D$ whose columns generate all the relations on $f$. Thus $$\label{algebra:ideal-presentation}
 P^t\xrightarrow{D}P^s\xrightarrow{f}I\longrightarrow0$$ is exact. Define $$\psi:R^s\longrightarrow R^t,\qquad v\longmapsto v\bar D,
 \qquad K=\ker\psi,
 \qquad
 J=\sum_{j=1}^e R\,\overline{\partial_j f}.$$ Differentiating $fD=0$ and reducing modulo $I$ shows that $J\subset K$. Right-exact reduction of <a href="#algebra:ideal-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ideal-presentation">[algebra:ideal-presentation]</a> gives a presentation of $I/I^2$; its dual therefore identifies $$\label{algebra:conormal-dual}
 K=\mathop{\mathrm{Hom}}_R(I/I^2,R),\qquad T^1=K/J.$$ In particular, no exactness on the left after reduction has been assumed.

<a id="stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy"></a>
<div id="algebra:finite-determinacy" class="lemma">

**Lemma 16** (Finite determinacy with a relation matrix). *Suppose that $T^1$ in <a href="#algebra:conormal-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:conormal-dual">[algebra:conormal-dual]</a> has finite length. There exist integers $N,n_0$ depending only on $f,D$ such that the following holds. If a row $g\in P^s$ and an $s\times t$ matrix $B$ satisfy $$gB=0,\qquad
 g-f\in\mathfrak n^{n_0}P^s,\qquad
 B-D\in\mathfrak n^NP^{s\times t},$$ then there are a continuous $\mathbb C$-algebra automorphism $\Phi$ of $P$ and an invertible matrix $U\in\operatorname{GL}_s(P)$ such that $$f=\Phi(g)U.$$ One can take $\Phi(x_j)\equiv x_j\pmod{\mathfrak n^2}$ and $U\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak n}$. Consequently $P/(g)\simeq P/(f)$ as complete local $\mathbb C$-algebras.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy"><summary>Proof of stmt-algebra-finite-determinacy</summary>

*Proof.* We improve the equations one order at a time. The exact relation $gB=0$ makes the equation error, reduced modulo $I$, nearly belong to $K$. Artin–Rees lets us correct it into $K$; the finite length of $K/J$ then expresses the sufficiently high-order corrected class by coordinate derivatives. A change of generators removes the remaining $I$-valued error. We arrange these corrections so that the equation precision increases at every step while the relation precision stays fixed. The resulting coordinate and generator changes converge in $P$.

<a id="algebra:ar-image"></a>
<a id="algebra:ar-ideal"></a>
We first choose the fixed bounds. For a map $u:M\to M'$ of finite modules over a Noetherian local ring with maximal ideal $\mathfrak a$, the Artin–Rees lemma <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[StacksCompletion](#ref-StacksCompletion), Tag 00IN], applied to $\operatorname{im}u\subset M'$ gives a constant $a\ge0$ such that $$\operatorname{im}u\cap\mathfrak a^rM'
 \subset u(\mathfrak a^{r-a}M)\qquad(r\ge a).$$ Choose nonnegative constants $\alpha,\beta,d$ so that <a id="algebra:ar-kernel"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \operatorname{im}\psi\cap\mathfrak m_R^rR^t
 &\subset\psi(\mathfrak m_R^{r-\alpha}R^s),
 \label{algebra:ar-image}\\
 K\cap\mathfrak m_R^rR^s
 &\subset\mathfrak m_R^{r-\beta}K,
 \label{algebra:ar-kernel}\\
 I^{\oplus s}\cap\mathfrak n^rP^s
 &\subset\{fC:C\in\mathfrak n^{r-d}P^{s\times s}\}
 \label{algebra:ar-ideal}
\end{aligned}$$ whenever the corresponding exponents are nonnegative. In <a href="#algebra:ar-ideal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ar-ideal">[algebra:ar-ideal]</a>, Artin–Rees is applied to the map $P^{s\times s}\to P^s$, $C\mapsto fC$, with image $I^{\oplus s}$; it is the filtration induced from $P^s$ that is relevant.

<a id="algebra:linear-correction"></a>
Choose $\ell\ge0$ with $\mathfrak m_R^\ell K\subset J$ and set <a id="algebra:constants"></a>$$\label{algebra:constants}
 c_0=\beta+\ell,\qquad c=c_0+d,\qquad
 N\ge\alpha+1,\qquad
 n_0\ge\max\{c+N,c+2,2c+1\}.$$ We first describe one improvement step. Suppose $n\ge n_0$ and $$h=g-f\in\mathfrak n^nP^s,
 \qquad B-D\in\mathfrak n^NP^{s\times t},
 \qquad gB=0.$$ The exact relation, reduced modulo the fixed ideal $I$, gives <a id="algebra:high-order-relation"></a>$$\label{algebra:high-order-relation}
 \bar h\bar D
 =\bar g\bar D
 =-\bar g(\bar B-\bar D)
 =-\bar h(\bar B-\bar D)
 \in\mathfrak m_R^{n+N}R^t.$$ The factor $\bar h$ on the right is what provides order $n+N$. By <a href="#algebra:ar-image" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ar-image">[algebra:ar-image]</a>, there is $$\bar\epsilon\in\mathfrak m_R^{n+N-\alpha}R^s
 \subset\mathfrak m_R^{n+1}R^s,
 \qquad \psi(\bar\epsilon)=\psi(\bar h).$$ Hence $$\kappa=\bar h-\bar\epsilon
 \in K\cap\mathfrak m_R^nR^s
 \subset\mathfrak m_R^{n-\beta}K
 \subset\mathfrak m_R^{n-c_0}J.$$ Here the last inclusion uses $\mathfrak m_R^\ell K\subset J$, with the remaining factor of order $n-\beta-\ell=n-c_0$. Lift coefficients expressing $\kappa$ in the displayed generators of $J$ to elements $a_j\in\mathfrak n^{n-c_0}$, and lift $\bar\epsilon$ to $\epsilon\in\mathfrak n^{n+1}P^s$. Then $$w=h-\sum_{j=1}^e a_j\partial_jf-\epsilon
 \in I^{\oplus s}\cap\mathfrak n^{n-c_0}P^s.$$ The order of this ideal-valued remainder need only be $n-c_0$, since the lifted derivative combination may have lower order than $h$: a derivative of a defining equation can be a unit. Using <a href="#algebra:ar-ideal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ar-ideal">[algebra:ar-ideal]</a>, write $w=fC$ with $C\in\mathfrak n^{n-c}P^{s\times s}$. We have proved $$\label{algebra:linear-correction}
 h=\sum_{j=1}^e a_j\partial_jf+fC+\epsilon,
 \qquad
 a_j,C\in\mathfrak n^{n-c},
 \qquad \epsilon\in\mathfrak n^{n+1}P^s.$$ Thus the loss $d$ for lifting the ideal-valued remainder has been included in the fixed constant $c$.

<a id="algebra:one-step"></a>
Put $q=n-c$, and define $$\label{algebra:one-step}
 \phi(x_j)=x_j-a_j,\qquad M=(1+C)^{-1},\qquad
 g_{\mathrm{new}}=\phi(g)M,\qquad
 B_{\mathrm{new}}=(1+C)\phi(B).$$ Our bounds ensure $$q\ge\max\{N,2\},\qquad 2q\ge n+1,
 \qquad n+q-1\ge n+1.$$ In particular, $\phi$ is a formal automorphism with identity linear part, and $M$ is invertible. Formal Taylor expansion gives $$\phi(f)-f+\sum_j a_j\partial_jf\in\mathfrak n^{2q}P^s,
 \qquad
 \phi(h)-h\in\mathfrak n^{n+q-1}P^s.$$ Combining these estimates with <a href="#algebra:linear-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:linear-correction">[algebra:linear-correction]</a> gives $$\phi(g)\equiv f(1+C)\pmod{\mathfrak n^{n+1}P^s},
 \qquad
 g_{\mathrm{new}}-f\in\mathfrak n^{n+1}P^s.$$ The relations in <a href="#algebra:one-step" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:one-step">[algebra:one-step]</a> transform exactly: $$g_{\mathrm{new}}B_{\mathrm{new}}
 =\phi(g)(1+C)^{-1}(1+C)\phi(B)=0.$$ Moreover, substitution by $\phi$ changes any power series by an element of $\mathfrak n^q$, and $C$ has order at least $q$. Therefore $$B_{\mathrm{new}}-D
 =C\phi(B)+(\phi(B)-B)+(B-D)
 \in\mathfrak n^NP^{s\times t}.$$ The fixed relation precision $N$ is preserved, while the equation precision increases from $n$ to $n+1$.

Starting with $(g_{n_0},B_{n_0})=(g,B)$, repeat this step for $n=n_0,n_0+1,\ldots$. Write $\phi_n,M_n$ for its corrections. For completeness, the cumulative coordinate and generator changes obey $$\Phi_{n_0}=\mathop{\mathrm{id}},\quad U_{n_0}=1,\qquad
 \Phi_{n+1}=\phi_n\circ\Phi_n,\qquad
 U_{n+1}=\phi_n(U_n)M_n,$$ so that $g_n=\Phi_n(g)U_n$ at every stage. Since $$(\phi_n-\mathop{\mathrm{id}})(P)\subset\mathfrak n^{n-c},
 \qquad M_n-1\in\mathfrak n^{n-c}P^{s\times s},$$ the consecutive images $\Phi_n(x_j)$ and the consecutive matrices $U_n$ differ in orders tending to infinity. They converge in the complete ring $P$ to $\Phi(x_j)$ and $U$. The resulting substitution $\Phi$ has identity linear part and hence is an automorphism, while $U\equiv1\pmod{\mathfrak n}$ is invertible. Taking limits in $g_n=\Phi_n(g)U_n$ yields $f=\Phi(g)U$, as required. ◻

</details>

The original matrix $D$ must generate all the relations, in order that <a href="#algebra:conormal-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:conormal-dual">[algebra:conormal-dual]</a> hold. No such assertion is needed for the approximating matrix $B$: its exact zero product and its closeness to $D$ are sufficient. Thus Lemma <a href="#algebra:finite-determinacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:finite-determinacy">16</a> concerns solutions of the finite system $gB=0$, not arbitrary truncations of a list of defining equations.

<a id="pm-h-32"></a>
#### The algebraic neighborhood

<a id="stmt-prop-algebraization"></a>
<div id="prop:algebraization" class="proposition">

**Proposition 17** (Algebraization of an isolated analytic germ). *Let $(X,x)$ be a normal complex analytic germ of dimension $d\ge1$, smooth away from $x$, and suppose that its holomorphic tangent module is free of rank $d$. There exist a finitely generated normal integral $\mathbb C$-algebra $A$ and a maximal ideal $\mathfrak p\subset A$ such that $$\dim A=d,\qquad A/\mathfrak p=\mathbb C,\qquad
 \widehat{A_{\mathfrak p}}\simeq\widehat{\mathcal O_{X,x}},\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^d.$$ The affine variety $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ can be chosen smooth away from $\mathfrak p$. If $x$ is singular, $A_{\mathfrak p}$ is nonregular.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-algebraization"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-algebraization</summary>

<a id="algebra:completion-invariants"></a>
*Proof.* *The completed analytic presentation.* Embed the germ in a smooth analytic germ and write $$S=\mathcal O_{X,x}=\mathbb C\{x_1,\ldots,x_e\}/I_{\mathrm{an}}.$$ Choose a finite row $f$ generating $I_{\mathrm{an}}$ and a finite matrix $D$ generating all its relations over the convergent power-series ring. Completion is exact on finite modules, so they give the presentation <a href="#algebra:ideal-presentation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:ideal-presentation">[algebra:ideal-presentation]</a> over $P=\mathbb C[[x_1,\ldots,x_e]]$, with $R=\widehat S=P/(f)$. Complex analytic local rings are excellent <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Cutkosky2017](#ref-Cutkosky2017), Proposition 2.1(1)]; thus the completion of the normal local ring $S$ is a normal local domain. Also $$\label{algebra:completion-invariants}
 \dim R=\dim S=d,
 \qquad R\text{ is regular if and only if }S\text{ is regular}.$$ We use here the usual exactness and permanence properties of completion <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[StacksCompletion](#ref-StacksCompletion)].

The convergent version of <a href="#algebra:conormal-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:conormal-dual">[algebra:conormal-dual]</a> is the conormal dual of the analytic embedding. On the smooth locus the tangent-to-normal map is surjective: a smooth analytic subspace of a complex manifold is locally a regular embedding. Its cokernel is consequently a coherent module supported at the isolated point $x$, and has finite length. Flat completion identifies this cokernel with the module $T^1=K/J$ above. Lemma <a href="#algebra:finite-determinacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:finite-determinacy">16</a> therefore applies to $f,D$.

<a id="algebra:continuous-tangent"></a>
We also record precisely which differentials are being completed. The module of holomorphic differentials of $S$ has the finite Jacobian presentation with generators $dx_j$ and relations $df_i$. Its dual is the holomorphic tangent module. Flat completion of this kernel yields $$\label{algebra:continuous-tangent}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Der}}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{\mathbb C}(R,R)
 =\ker\left(R^e\longrightarrow R^s,
       (v_j)\longmapsto\sum_{j=1}^e v_j\overline{\partial_jf}\right)
 \simeq R^d.$$ Here “continuous” refers to the maximal-ideal topology. A continuous derivation of a power-series quotient is determined by its values on the $x_j$, and the displayed relations are precisely the conditions for it to descend from $P$. All completed differential modules used below are these finite continuous differential modules.

<a id="algebra:artin-system"></a>
<a id="algebra:formal-equivalence"></a>
*Approximation of equations and relations.* Let $$H=\bigl(\mathbb C[x_1,\ldots,x_e]_{(x_1,\ldots,x_e)}\bigr)^h$$ be the henselization, whose completion is $P$. This is precisely a henselization of a finite-type algebra over the field $\mathbb C$ at a prime, as covered by Artin’s Theorem 1.10. Apply that approximation theorem over $H$ to the polynomial system in the $s+st$ entries of a row $G$ and a matrix $B$, $$\label{algebra:artin-system}
 \sum_{i=1}^s G_i B_{i\nu}=0\qquad(1\le\nu\le t),$$ with formal solution $(G,B)=(f,D)$. It gives a row $g$ and a matrix $B$ with entries in $H$, satisfying $gB=0$ exactly and agreeing with $f,D$ to any prescribed finite order <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Artin1969](#ref-Artin1969), Theorem 1.10]; see also <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[StacksApproximation](#ref-StacksApproximation)]. Choose that order at least $\max\{n_0,N\}$, with $n_0,N$ from Lemma <a href="#algebra:finite-determinacy" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="algebra:finite-determinacy">16</a>. The lemma gives $$\label{algebra:formal-equivalence}
 P/(g)\simeq R.$$

<a id="algebra:completed-local-ring"></a>
The henselization is the filtered colimit of pointed étale neighborhoods of the origin, with the same residue field <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[StacksHenselization](#ref-StacksHenselization)]. Thus the finitely many entries of $g,B$ are represented on a single such neighborhood. After passing to a later neighborhood, the finitely many identities <a href="#algebra:artin-system" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:artin-system">[algebra:artin-system]</a> hold there as well. If this stage is expressed over the local polynomial ring, finite presentation and clearing finitely many denominators spread it to a neighborhood of the origin in affine space; shrinking preserves the marked point and makes the morphism étale. After an affine shrinking the identities hold in its coordinate ring. We obtain an affine étale neighborhood $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}E\to\mathbb A^e_{\mathbb C}$ with a marked point $\mathfrak q$ over the origin, residue field $\mathbb C$, and a row $g$ of elements of $E$ vanishing at $\mathfrak q$. Its completed ambient local ring is $P$. Put $$A'=E/(g),\qquad \mathfrak p'=\mathfrak q/(g),\qquad
 T=A'_{\mathfrak p'}.$$ Then $A'$ is of finite type over $\mathbb C$, and exactness of completion together with <a href="#algebra:formal-equivalence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:formal-equivalence">[algebra:formal-equivalence]</a> gives $$\label{algebra:completed-local-ring}
 \widehat T=P/(g)\simeq R.$$ In particular, this construction preserves the complete local ring, including its dimension and its normality.

<a id="algebra:tangent-descent"></a>
*Descent of tangent freeness and local ring properties.* Because the ambient neighborhood is étale over $\mathbb A^e_{\mathbb C}$, its differentials have basis $dx_1,\ldots,dx_e$ near the marked point. Completing the finite presentation of $\Omega^1_{T/\mathbb C}$ therefore gives the continuous Jacobian presentation of $P/(g)$. For a finitely presented module, flat base change commutes with its dual: this follows by expressing the dual as the kernel of a map between finite free modules. The continuous chain rule under <a href="#algebra:completed-local-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:completed-local-ring">[algebra:completed-local-ring]</a> and <a href="#algebra:continuous-tangent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:continuous-tangent">[algebra:continuous-tangent]</a> consequently give $$\label{algebra:tangent-descent}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(T)\otimes_T\widehat T
 \simeq\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}^{\mathrm{cont}}_{\mathbb C}(\widehat T,\widehat T)
 \simeq\widehat T^{d}.$$ This is an equality obtained from finite presentations; it does not identify ordinary algebraic differentials of a power-series ring with its continuous differentials.

Let $M=\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(T)$. By <a href="#algebra:tangent-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:tangent-descent">[algebra:tangent-descent]</a>, its residue vector space has dimension $d$. Lift a basis to $d$ elements of $M$. Nakayama’s lemma gives a surjection $T^d\to M$, which after completion is a surjection between free modules of rank $d$ over the local ring $\widehat T$, and hence an isomorphism. Faithful flatness of completion annihilates its kernel, proving $M\simeq T^d$.

The ring $T$ injects into its domain completion, so it is a domain. Normality also descends. Indeed, if $a/b\in\operatorname{Frac}(T)$ is integral over $T$, it is integral over the normal domain $\widehat T$ and hence belongs to $\widehat T$. Thus $a\in b\widehat T\cap T=bT$, where the last equality follows from faithful flatness, and $a/b\in T$. Finally, completion preserves both Krull dimension and the dimension of the maximal ideal modulo its square. Equations <a href="#algebra:completion-invariants" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:completion-invariants">[algebra:completion-invariants]</a> and <a href="#algebra:completed-local-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:completed-local-ring">[algebra:completed-local-ring]</a> show that $T$ has dimension $d$ and is nonregular whenever $S$ is nonregular.

<a id="algebra:isolated-descent"></a>
The isolated-singularity property can also be retained. Let $\mathfrak j_{\mathrm{an}}$ be the $d$th Fitting ideal of the holomorphic differential module of $S$. The analytic Jacobian criterion identifies its zero set with the nonsmooth locus, so some power of the maximal ideal of $S$ is contained in $\mathfrak j_{\mathrm{an}}$; in the smooth case this ideal is the unit ideal. Fitting ideals commute with base change. The completed Jacobian presentations and <a href="#algebra:completed-local-ring" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:completed-local-ring">[algebra:completed-local-ring]</a> thus imply, by contraction from the faithfully flat completion, that $$\label{algebra:isolated-descent}
 \mathfrak m_T^b\subset
 \operatorname{Fitt}_d(\Omega^1_{T/\mathbb C})
 \quad\text{for some }b\ge0.$$ On the reduced pure-$d$-dimensional neighborhood constructed below, the algebraic Jacobian criterion interprets this containment as nonsmoothness supported at the marked point.

*An integral normal affine neighborhood with a free tangent module.* The finite module $\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A')$ localizes at $\mathfrak p'$ to $\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(T)$, because $\Omega^1_{A'/\mathbb C}$ is finitely presented. Represent the basis just obtained after inverting an element outside $\mathfrak p'$. It defines a map from a free module of rank $d$ to the derivation module whose kernel and cokernel vanish at $\mathfrak p'$. Their supports are closed, so a further principal shrinking makes this map an isomorphism.

Since $T$ is a domain, exactly one irreducible component of $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A'$ passes through $\mathfrak p'$, and the support of the nilradical avoids $\mathfrak p'$. Remove the other components and that support. The remaining neighborhood is reduced and irreducible. Its normal locus is open, since schemes of finite type over $\mathbb C$ are excellent, and contains $\mathfrak p'$ by the normality of $T$; restrict to this normal locus as well. This irreducible neighborhood has dimension $d$, since its marked point is closed and its local ring has dimension $d$. Its nonsmooth locus is closed and, by <a href="#algebra:isolated-descent" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="algebra:isolated-descent">[algebra:isolated-descent]</a>, has no positive-dimensional component through $\mathfrak p'$. Remove any components of that locus that do not contain $\mathfrak p'$.

All the restrictions just made are open neighborhoods of $\mathfrak p'$. Choose a principal affine neighborhood contained in their intersection, and write it as $\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$, with marked maximal ideal $\mathfrak p$. The algebra $A$ is finitely generated, normal and integral, its dimension is $d$, and $$A_{\mathfrak p}=T,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^d.$$ The marked residue field and completed local ring are unchanged, and the variety is smooth away from that point. This proves all the assertions. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-algebraization-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-algebraization</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Proposition <a href="#prop:analytic-counterexample" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:analytic-counterexample">14</a> supplies a singular normal complex analytic surface germ, smooth away from its marked point, whose holomorphic tangent module is free of rank two. Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:algebraization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:algebraization">17</a> and fix any of the approximations and affine neighborhoods furnished by its proof. Its coordinate ring $A$ is a finitely generated normal integral $\mathbb C$-algebra of dimension two, with $$\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^2,
 \qquad A_{\mathfrak p}\text{ nonregular}.$$ Thus the derivation module is free, and in particular projective, whereas $A$ is not regular. This is the required counterexample over $\mathbb C$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-33"></a>
#### The smooth locus and derivation blowups

Let $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ be the affine surface just constructed, and let $U=X_{\mathrm{reg}}$ be its smooth locus. Recall that the logarithmic Kodaira dimension of a smooth quasiprojective surface is $$\overline\kappa(U)=\kappa(K_{\overline U}+D),$$ where $\overline U$ is a smooth projective compactification and its reduced boundary $D=\overline U\setminus U$ is a simple normal-crossing divisor. This value is independent of the compactification.

<a id="stmt-algebra-log-kodaira"></a>
<div id="algebra:log-kodaira" class="corollary">

**Corollary 18**. *The smooth locus $U$ of the affine surface $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ constructed in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> has $\overline\kappa(U)=2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-algebra-log-kodaira"><summary>Proof of stmt-algebra-log-kodaira</summary>

*Proof.* The free tangent sheaf on $X$ restricts to a trivial tangent bundle on $U$. If $\overline\kappa(U)\le1$, the theorem of Biswas, Gurjar and Kolte <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[BiswasGurjarKolte2014](#ref-BiswasGurjarKolte2014), Theorem 0.1(1)] would make the normal affine surface $X$ smooth, contrary to Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. The only remaining value of logarithmic Kodaira dimension for a surface is two. ◻

</details>

For a finite $A$-module $M$ of generic rank $r$, viewed as a coherent sheaf on $X$, its blowup is universal among proper birational morphisms from integral varieties to $X$ for which the pullback of $M$ modulo torsion is locally free of rank $r$. Every such morphism factors uniquely through the blowup. We use Villamayor’s description, as recalled in <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[BarajasChavezRomano2026](#ref-BarajasChavezRomano2026), 1.6, Theorem 1.17].

<a id="stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups"></a>
<div id="algebra:derivation-blowups" class="corollary">

**Corollary 19**. *Starting with the singular surface $X=\mathop{\mathrm{Spec}}A$ of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, every iteration of blowing up at the derivation module is isomorphic to $X$. The same holds if each blowup is followed by normalization. Neither procedure resolves its singular point.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups"><summary>Proof of stmt-algebra-derivation-blowups</summary>

*Proof.* Since $\mathop{\mathrm{Der}}_{\mathbb C}(A)\simeq A^2$, the identity of $X$ already has the universal property just stated. Its blowup at the derivation module is therefore $X$ itself, and the operation remains unchanged at every iteration. Normalization also leaves $X$ unchanged because $A$ is normal. The relation with the Lipman–Zariski conjecture was noted in <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[BarajasChavezRomano2026](#ref-BarajasChavezRomano2026), Introduction, after Question 1]; the surface above gives a negative answer to that question in characteristic zero. ◻

</details>
